Modelling Recursion and Probabilistic Choice in Guarded Type Theory
Philipp Jan Andries Stassen, Rasmus Ejlers Møgelberg, Maaike Zwart, Alejandro Aguirre, Lars Birkedal

Abstract
. Constructive type theory combines logic and programming in one language. This is useful both for reasoning about programs written in type theory, as well as for reasoning about other programming languages inside type theory. It is well-known that it is challenging to extend these applications to languages with recursion and computational effects such as probabilistic choice, because these features are not easily represented in constructive type theory. We show how to define and reason about 𝖥𝖯𝖢 ⊕ \mathsf{FPC}_{\oplus} , a programming language with probabilistic choice and recursive types, in guarded type theory. We use higher inductive types to represent finite distributions and guarded recursion to model recursion. We define both operational and denotational semantics of 𝖥𝖯𝖢 ⊕ \mathsf{FPC}_{\oplus} , as well as a relation between the two. The relation can be used to prove adequacy, but we also show how to use it to reason about programs up to contextual equivalence.



DOI:
XXXXXXX.XXXXXXX

1.
Introduction

Traditionally, modelling and reasoning about programming languages is done using either operational or denotational techniques.
Denotational semantics provides mathematical abstractions that are used to see beyond the details of the operational implementation, to
describe principles common to many different languages, and to provide modular building blocks that can be extended to settings with more language features.
On the other hand, denotational semantics can easily become very sophisticated mathematically, and modelling combinations of recursion and other features such as probabilistic sampling and higher-order store can be difficult.
Operational techniques are often more direct and flexible, and are usually easier to implement in proof assistants,
but reasoning requires constructing complex operational models and logics.

Synthetic guarded domain theory
(SGDT) provides an alternative approach
to both denotational and operational semantics.
The idea is to work in an expressive meta-language with guarded recursion in the sense of Nakano
(
Nakano, 2000
)
,
and to use recursion on the meta-level to model recursion on the object level.
The specific meta-language used
in this paper is Clocked Cubical Type Theory (CCTT)
(
Kristensen et al., 2022
)
.
It includes a modal type-operator
\triangleright^{\kappa}
, indexed by a so-called clock
\kappa
(see Section
2
), to describe data that is available
one time step from now. It is possible to define elements by guarded recursion by means of a fixed point combinator
\mathsf{fix}^{\kappa}:(\triangleright^{\kappa}A\to A)\to A
.
By applying the fixed point combinator to an operator on a universe one can obtain solutions to guarded recursive type equations
(
Birkedal and Møgelberg, 2013
)
.
For example, one can define a
guarded delay monad

{L^{\kappa}}
, which maps a type
A
to
{L^{\kappa}}A
satisfying
{L^{\kappa}}A\simeq A+\triangleright^{\kappa}({L^{\kappa}}A)
.
This guarded delay monad has been used to model recursive computations, both
in operational semantics, as well as in denotational semantics, which simply models function types of the object language using the Kleisli functions
of the metalanguage:
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle A\to B$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}\triangleq\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle A$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}\to{L^{\kappa}}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle B$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}
. Guarded recursion is then also used for reasoning about the model, in particular
to establish a relation between syntax and semantics to prove adequacy. These results were originally proved for the simply typed
lambda calculus extended with recursive terms
(
Paviotti et al., 2015
)

and recursive types
(
Møgelberg and Paviotti, 2019
)
, but have been recently extended to cover also languages with general store and
polymorphism
(
Sterling et al., 2022
)
.

SGDT has several of the benefits listed for operational and denotational techniques above: the models can be described at a high level of
abstraction, and once one is familiar with programming in the meta-language,
the mathematical knowledge required for constructing the models is limited. The main reason for both of these is that recursion and other tools needed for constructing and reasoning about these models, are build into the meta-language,
so the mathematical difficulties appear on the next meta-level. Moreover, since metalanguages such as CCTT are dependent type theories, these results
can potentially be directly implemented in proof assistants. For example, CCTT has been implemented
1
1

1

https://github.com/agda/guarded

as an experimental extension of Cubical Agda
(
Vezzosi et al., 2019
)
.
Finally, the denotational semantics of the language can be seen as a shallow embedding
of the object language, so one can potentially also use the meta-language as a language for both programming and reasoning.

In this paper we show how to extend SGDT to model probabilistic programming languages, i.e.,
languages that include commands that generate random values by sampling from a probability distribution.
It is well known that it is challenging to develop semantic models for reasoning about higher-order probabilistic programming languages
that include recursion, even in a classical meta-theory.
Nonetheless, a plethora of denotational approaches have been investigated in recent years,
(
Jones and Plotkin, 1989
;
Vákár et al., 2019
;
Heunen et al., 2017
;
Ehrhard et al., 2018
;
Dahlqvist and Kozen, 2020
)
.
Other operational-based approaches to reason about probabilistic programs have also been shown to scale to rich languages with a variety of features,
using techniques such as logical relations
(
Johann et al., 2010
;
Bizjak and Birkedal, 2015
;
Culpepper and Cobb, 2017
;
Wand et al., 2018
;
Zhang and Amin, 2022
)
, or bisimulations
(
Crubillé and Lago, 2014
;
Lago et al., 2014
)
.

Precisely, we show how to develop operational and denotational semantics of

\mathsf{FPC}_{\oplus}
2
2

2

The name derives from Plotkin’s Fixed Point Calculus (FPC)
(
Plotkin, 1985
;
Fiore, 1994
)
, a call-by-value higher-order probabilistic programming language with recursive types,
in CCTT, and prove that the denotational semantics is adequate with respect to the operational semantics. We also show how to use these results for reasoning about
\mathsf{FPC}_{\oplus}
programs
in CCTT. One of the challenges for doing so is that most of the previously developed theory for probabilistic languages relies on classical reasoning, which is not available in CCTT.
For example, many types used in our model do not have decidable equality, and as a consequence, even the finite distribution monad
\mathcal{D}{}
cannot be defined in the standard
classical way. Fortunately, CCTT not only models guarded recursion but also higher-inductive types (HITs), which
we use to define
\mathcal{D}{}
: on a set
A
,
\mathcal{D}{A}

is the free convex algebra
(
Jacobs, 2010
)
, that is, a set
\mathcal{D}{A}
together with a
binary operation
{}\oplus_{p}{}
, indexed by a rational number
p
, satisfying
the natural equational theory (idempotency, associativity, and commutativity).
Using a HIT to represent distributions
allows us to use the equational properties of the meta-language level when
reasoning about the semantics of
\mathsf{FPC}_{\oplus}
, and it provides us with a useful induction principle for
proving propositions ranging over
\mathcal{D}{A}
.

To model the combination of recursion and probabilistic choice,we use the

guarded convex delay monad
which we denote by
\mathsf{D}^{\kappa}{}
. On a type
A
,

\mathsf{D}^{\kappa}{A}\simeq\mathcal{D}(A+\triangleright^{\kappa}{(\mathsf{D}^{\kappa}{A})})
. Intuitively, this means that a computation of type
A
will be a distribution over values of
A
(immediately
available) and delayed computations of type
A
. Quantifying over clocks gives the
convex delay monad

\mathsf{D}^{\!\forall\!}A\triangleq\forall\kappa.\mathsf{D}^{\kappa}A
which is a coinductive solution to the equation

\mathsf{D}^{\!\forall\!}A\simeq\mathcal{D}(A+\mathsf{D}^{\!\forall\!}A)
. Both operational and denotational semantics
are defined using
\mathsf{D}^{\kappa}{}
because it allows for guarded recursive definitions. These definitions
can then be clock quantified to give elements of
\mathsf{D}^{\!\forall\!}{}
, which has the benefit that steps
can be eliminated, because
\triangleright^{\kappa}
does not get in the way.

As our main result, we define a logical relation, relating
the denotational and operational semantics, and prove that it is sound with
respect to contextual refinement. Traditionally, defining a logical relation
relating denotational and operational semantics for a language with recursive
types is non-trivial, see, e.g.,
(
Pitts, 1996
)
. Here we use
the guarded type theory to define the logical relation by guarded recursion. As
usual, the logical relation is divided into a relation for values and a relation
for computations. Since computations compute to distributions, the challenge
here lies in defining the relation for computations in terms of the relation for
values. Earlier work on operationally-based logical relations

(
Bizjak and Birkedal, 2015
;
Aguirre and Birkedal, 2023
)
used
bi-orthogonality to reduce the problem to relating termination probabilities for
computations of ground type. Here, we follow the approach of the recent
article
(
Gregersen et al., 2024
)
, which uses
couplings
(
Lindvall, 2002
;
Thorisson, 2000
;
Villani, 2008
;
Barthe et al., 2009
)

to lift relations on values to relations on distributions. Note that the
development in
(
Gregersen et al., 2024
)
relies on classical logic (in particular,
the composition of couplings, the so-called bind lemma, relies on the axiom of
choice) and thus does not apply here. Instead, we define a novel constructive
notion of lifting of relations
{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}:A\to B\to\mathsf{Prop}
to relations on convex delay
algebras
{}\,{}\overline{{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}}^{\kappa}\,{}:\mathsf{D}^{\kappa}{A}\to\mathsf{D}^{\!\forall\!}{B}\to\mathsf{Prop}
by guarded recursion.
We establish a series of basic results for this, including a version of the important bind lemma that
allows us to compose these liftings in proofs.

Finally, we use the semantics and the logical relation to prove contextual refinement of examples that combine probabilistic choice and recursion.
The examples illustrate yet another benefit of the denotational semantics: Since fewer steps are needed in the denotational semantics
than in the operational semantics, using this for reasoning makes the arguments less cluttered by steps than a direct
relation between syntax and syntax would.

Contributions

(1)

To the best of our knowledge, we present the first constructive type theoretic account of
operational and denotational semantics of
\mathsf{FPC}_{\oplus}
.

(2)

We develop the theory of the finite distribution monad in cubical type theory, and we introduce the convex delay monad in CCTT,
and develop the basic theory for it.

(3)

We develop the basic constructive theory of couplings for convex delay algebras and use it to define a logical relation, relating denotational and operational semantics.

(4)

We demonstrate how to use the semantics to reason about examples that combine probabilistic choice and recursion.

2.
Clocked Cubical Type Theory

We will work in Clocked Cubical Type Theory (CCTT)
(
Kristensen et al., 2022
)
, an extension of Cubical Type Theory
(
Cohen et al., 2017
)

with guarded recursion. At present, CCTT is the only existing type theory containing all constructions needed for this paper.
We will not describe CCTT in detail, but only
describe the properties we need,
with the hope of making the paper more accessible, and
so that the results can be reused in other (future) type theories with the same properties.

2.1.
Basic properties and HITs

CCTT has an infinite hierarchy of (Tarski style) universes (
\mathsf{U}_{i}
), as well as identity types satisfying
the standard rules and function extensionality. Precisely, CCTT, being based on Cubical Type Theory,
has a path type as primitive, rather than an identity type in the traditional sense. However,
the differences between the two are inessential to this work.
We will write
a=_{A}b
, or just
a=b
,
for the identity type associated with terms
a,b:A
of the same type.
Following conventions from homotopy type
theory
(
Univalent Foundations Program, 2013
)
, we say that a type
A
is a (homotopy) proposition if for
any
x,y:A
, the type
x=y
is contractible, and that it is a (homotopy) set if for any

x,y:A
, the type
x=y
is a proposition. We write
\mathsf{Prop}_{i}
, and
\mathsf{Set}_{i}

for the subuniverses of
\mathsf{U}_{i}
of propositions and sets respectively, often omitting the
universe level
i
. We write
A\simeq B
for the type of equivalences
from
A
to
B
. We shall mostly use this in the case where
A
and
B
are sets, and in that
case, an equivalence is simply given by the standard notion of isomorphism of sets as phrased
inside type theory using propositional equality. All types used represent to syntax, as well as the denotation
of all types, are sets. Likewise all relations are valued in
\mathsf{Prop}
. These choices simplify reasoning,
as no higher dimensional structure needs to be accounted for. The only types used that are not sets
are the universes
\mathsf{Set}
and
\mathsf{U}
.

CCTT also has higher inductive types (HITs), and we will use these to construct
propositional truncation, set truncation and the finite distributions monad (see
section 3
).
In particular, this means that one can express ordinary propositional logic with operators
\land,\lor,\exists,\forall

on
\mathsf{Prop}
, using the encodings of
\exists
and
\vee
defined using propositional truncation
(
Univalent Foundations Program, 2013
)
.
Recall in particular the elimination principles for
\exists
: When proving a proposition
\psi

assuming
\exists(x:X).\phi(x)
we may assume we have an
x
in hand satisfying
\phi(x)
, but
we cannot do that when mapping from
\exists(x:X).\phi(x)
to an arbitrary type.
We will also need inductive types to represent the type
\mathbb{N}
of natural numbers,
as well as types and terms of the language
\mathsf{FPC}_{\oplus}
described in
section 5
.
These are all captured by the schema for higher inductive types in CCTT
(
Kristensen et al., 2022
)
.

2.2.
Guarded recursion

\frac{\Gamma\vdash\quad\kappa:\mathsf{clock}\in\Gamma}{\Gamma,\alpha:\kappa\vdash{}}\frac{\Gamma\vdash t:{\triangleright}\,(\alpha\!:\!\kappa).A\quad\Gamma,\beta:\kappa,\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash}{\Gamma,\beta:\kappa,\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash t\,[\beta]:A\left[\beta/\alpha\right]}\frac{\Gamma,\alpha:\kappa\vdash t:A}{\Gamma\vdash\char 12\relax{\alpha}{\kappa}t:{\triangleright}\,(\alpha\!:\!\kappa).A}\frac{\Gamma,\kappa:\mathsf{clock}\vdash t:A}{\Gamma\vdash\Lambda\kappa.t:\forall\kappa.A}\frac{\Gamma\vdash t:\forall\kappa.A\quad\Gamma\vdash\kappa^{\prime}:\mathsf{clock}}{\Gamma\vdash t[\kappa^{\prime}]:A[\kappa^{\prime}/\kappa]}\frac{\Gamma\vdash t:\triangleright^{\kappa}A\to A}{\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{dfix}^{\kappa}\,t:\triangleright^{\kappa}A}\frac{\Gamma\vdash t:\triangleright^{\kappa}A\to A}{\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{pfix}^{\kappa}\,t:{\triangleright}\,(\alpha\!:\!\kappa).{{(\mathsf{dfix}^{\kappa}t)\,[\alpha]}=_{A}{t(\mathsf{dfix}^{\kappa}t)}}}

Figure 1.
Selected typing rules for Clocked Cubical Type Theory.

Guarded recursion uses a modal operator
\triangleright
(pronounced ‘later’) to describe data that is available one time step from now.
In multiclocked guarded recursion,
\triangleright
is indexed by a clock
\kappa
. Clocks can be variables of
the pretype
\mathsf{clock}
or they can be the clock constant
\kappa_{0}
. Clocks can be universally quantified in the type

\forall\kappa.A
, with rules similar to
\Pi
-types, including a functional extensionality principle.
The modality
\triangleright
is a Fitch-style modality
(
Clouston, 2018
;
Clouston et al., 2020
)
, in the sense that introduction and elimination for

\triangleright
is by abstraction and application to
ticks
, i.e., assumptions of the form
\alpha:\kappa
, to be thought
of as evidence that time has ticked on clock
\kappa
. Because ticks can occur in terms, they can also occur in
types, and the type
{\triangleright}\,(\alpha\!:\!\kappa).A
binds
\alpha
in
A
. When
\alpha
does not occur in
A
we simply
write
\triangleright^{\kappa}A
for
{\triangleright}\,(\alpha\!:\!\kappa).A
. The rules for tick abstraction and application that we shall
use in this paper are presented in Figure
1
. In the figure, the notation
\Gamma\vdash
means
that
\Gamma
is a well-formed context. Note that the rule for tick application assumes that

\beta
(or anything occurring after that in the context) does not already occur in
t
. This is to avoid terms of the type

\triangleright^{\kappa}\triangleright^{\kappa}A\to\triangleright^{\kappa}A
merging two time steps into one.
We will sometimes use the notation

(1)

\mathsf{next}^{\kappa}\triangleq\lambda x.\char 12\relax\alpha\kappa x:A\to\triangleright^{\kappa}A.

The rules presented in Figure
1
are special cases of those of CCTT. The general rules allow
certain ‘timeless’ assumptions in
\Gamma^{\prime}
to occur in
t
in the tick application rule. This allows typing of
an extensionality principle for
\triangleright
of type

(2)

\left(x=_{\triangleright^{\kappa}A}y\right)\simeq{\triangleright}\,(\alpha\!:\!\kappa).{\left({x\,[\alpha]}=_{A}{y\,[\alpha]}\right)}.

In this paper we shall simply take this as an axiom.
Intuitively, (
2
) states that
x
and
y
are equal,
if the elements they deliver in the next time step are equal.
One consequence of this
is that
\triangleright
preserves the property of being a set or a proposition.
Other omitted rules for ticks allow typing of
a
tick-irrelevance
axiom

(3)

\mathsf{tirr}^{\kappa}:\Pi(x:\triangleright^{\kappa}A).{\triangleright}\,(\alpha\!:\!\kappa).{\triangleright}\,(\beta\!:\!\kappa).{(x\,[\alpha])=_{A}(x\,[\beta])}.

stating that all ticks are propositionally equal (although they should not be considered judgementally equal
(
Bahr. et al., 2017
)
).

The fixed point combinator
\mathsf{dfix}{}
allows for defining and programming with guarded recursive types.
Define
\mathsf{fix}^{\kappa}:(\triangleright^{\kappa}A\to A)\to A
as
\mathsf{fix}^{\kappa}f=f(\mathsf{dfix}^{\kappa}f)
. Then one can prove that

(4)

\mathsf{fix}^{\kappa}t=t(\char 12\relax\alpha\kappa{\mathsf{fix}^{\kappa}t}).

Applying the fix point operator to maps on a universe, as in
(
Birkedal and Møgelberg, 2013
)
, one can define
guarded recursive types

such as the
guarded delay monad

{L^{\kappa}}
mapping a type
A
to
{L^{\kappa}}A
satisfying

(5)

{L^{\kappa}}A\simeq A+\triangleright^{\kappa}({L^{\kappa}}A).

In this paper, we will not spell out how such guarded recursive
types are defined as fixed points, but just give the defining guarded recursive equation. Intuitively, the reason
this is well defined is that
{L^{\kappa}}A
only occurs under a
\triangleright
on the right-hand side of (
5
), which
allows the recursive equation to be phrased as a map
\triangleright^{\kappa}\mathsf{U}\to\mathsf{U}
.
One can also use
\mathsf{fix}
to program with
{L^{\kappa}}
defining, e.g., the
diverging computation as
\bot=\mathsf{fix}(\lambda x.\mathsf{inr}(x))
(leaving the type equivalence between
{L^{\kappa}}A

and its unfolding implicit). In this case (
4
) specialises to
\bot=\mathsf{inr}(\char 12\relax\alpha\kappa\bot)
.

2.3.
Coinductive types

Coinductive types can be represented by quantifying over clocks in guarded recursive types
(
Atkey and McBride, 2013
)
.
For example, the type
{L}A\triangleq\forall\kappa.{L^{\kappa}}A
defines a coinductive solution to
{L}A\simeq A+{L}A
in
CCTT, provided
A
is
clock-irrelevant
, meaning that the canonical map
A\to\forall\kappa.A
is an equivalence.
More generally, one can prove that any functor
F:\mathsf{U}\to\mathsf{U}
(in the naive sense of having a functorial action

(A\to B)\to FA\to FB
) commuting with clock quantification in the sense that
F(\forall\kappa.X)\simeq\forall\kappa.F(X)

via the canonical map, has a final coalgebra defined as
\nu(F)\triangleq\forall\kappa.\nu^{\kappa}(F)
, where

\nu^{\kappa}(F)\simeq F(\triangleright^{\kappa}(\nu^{\kappa}(F))
is defined using
\mathsf{fix}
. This encoding also works
for indexed coinductive types. The correctness of the encoding of coinductive types can be proved in CCTT, and
relies on the type equivalence

\forall\kappa.A\simeq\forall\kappa.\triangleright^{\kappa}\!A.

For the encoding to be useful, one needs a large collection of clock-irrelevant types and functors commuting with clock
quantification. We will need that
\mathbb{N}
is clock-irrelevant, and that clock quantification commutes with sums
in the sense that
\forall\kappa.(A+B)\simeq(\forall\kappa.A)+(\forall\kappa.B)
. Both of these can be proved in
CCTT using the notion of induction under clocks for higher inductive types
(
Kristensen et al., 2022
)
. Note also that all propositions
P
are clock-irrelevant,
because the clock constant
\kappa_{0}
can be used to define a map
(\forall\kappa.P)\to P
. The following lemma can be used to prove types clock irrelevant.

Lemma 2.1.

Suppose
i:A\to B
is injective in the sense of the existence of a map

\Pi x,y:A.(i(x)=i(y))\to x=y
, and suppose
B
is clock irrelevant. Then
also
A
is clock-irrelevant.

For the rest of the paper, we work informally in CCTT.

3.
Finite distributions

In the previous section we saw the definition of the guarded delay monad
{L^{\kappa}}
(Equation
5
), which models non-terminating computations. In this section, we introduce the distribution monad
\mathcal{D}
, which models finite probability distributions. These two monads will be combined to form the guarded convex delay monad in the next section.

To construct the distribution monad
\mathcal{D}
, we first need some infrastructure to reason about probabilities.
We will assume types representing the open rational interval
(0,1)
and closed rational interval
[0,1]
,
and we assume that we have the operations product, division of smaller numbers by larger numbers, and inversion (
1-(-)
).
We also need that
(0,1)
is a set with decidable equality, and that
(0,1)
is clock irrelevant.
In practice, there are several ways of specifying
(0,1)
, for example as a type of pairs
(n,d)
, of mutually prime, positive natural numbers satisfying
n<d
, and
[0,1]
can be obtained, e.g., by just adding two points to
(0,1)
. For this encoding, clock irrelevance of
(0,1)
follows from the embedding into
\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}
and Lemma
2.1
. However, for this paper the specific implementation is irrelevant, so we leave this open.

Then, we need to find the right representation of probability distributions in type theory.
In classical presentations of probability theory, a finite distribution on a set
A
is a finite map into
[0,1]
, whose
values sum to
1
. In type theory, this would be represented by a subtype of
A\to[0,1]
, which would need some notion of finite support, as well as a definition of the functorial action of
\mathcal{D}
. Especially the latter is difficult to do: Given a map
f:A\rightarrow B
, then
\mathcal{D}(f):\mathcal{D}(A)\rightarrow\mathcal{D}(B)
should map a probability distribution
\mu
to the distribution which, for each
b:B
, sums the probabilities
\mu(a)
for each
a
such that
f(a)=b
. To define this, we would need decidable equality on
B
to compute for which
a
the equality
f(a)=b
holds, which is too restrictive.
Another approach could be to use lists of key-value pairs, but this requires a quotient to obtain the correct notion of equality of distributions, which would make it very hard to work with in practice.

Here we instead choose to represent
\mathcal{D}
as the free monad for the theory of convex algebras, which is known to generate the finite distribution monad
(
Jacobs, 2010
)
. The operation
\oplus
in the definition below should be thought of as a convex sum:
{x}\oplus_{p}{y}=p\cdot x+(1-p)\cdot y
.

Definition 3.1 (Convex Algebra).

A
convex algebra
is a set
A
together with an operation
\oplus:(0,1)\to A\to A\to A
, such that

(idem)

\displaystyle{\mu}\oplus_{p}{\mu}

\displaystyle=\mu

(comm)

\displaystyle{\mu}\oplus_{p}{\nu}

\displaystyle={\nu}\oplus_{1-p}{\mu}

(assoc)

\displaystyle{\left({\mu_{1}}\oplus_{p}{\mu_{2}}\right)}\oplus_{q}{\mu_{3}}

\displaystyle={\mu_{1}}\oplus_{pq}{\left({\mu_{2}}\oplus_{\frac{q-pq}{1-pq}}{\mu_{3}}\right)}

A map
f:A\to B
between convex algebras
A
and
B
is a
homomorphism
if

f({\mu}\oplus_{p}{\nu})={f(\mu)}\oplus_{p}{f(\nu)}
holds for all
\mu,\nu,p
.

Definition 3.2.

Let
\mathcal{D}(A)
be the higher inductive type defined using two constructors

\displaystyle\delta

\displaystyle:A\to\mathcal{D}(A)

\displaystyle\oplus:(0,1)\to\mathcal{D}(A)\to\mathcal{D}(A)\to\mathcal{D}(A),

and equations (paths) for idempotency, commutativity and associativity as in the definition of convex algebra, plus
an equation for set truncation.

One advantage of HITs is that they come with an easy induction principle. For
\mathcal{D}(A)
, it is as follows:
If
\phi(x)
is a proposition for all
x:\mathcal{D}(A)
and
\phi(\delta(a))
holds for all
a
, and moreover,
\phi(\mu)
and
\phi(\nu)
implies
\phi({\mu}\oplus_{p}{\nu})
for all
\mu,\nu,p
, then
\phi(x)
holds for all
x
. We will use this proof technique in numerous places throughout this paper. Similarly, the recursion principle for

\mathcal{D}(A)
states that to define a map
f
from
\mathcal{D}(A)
into a set
B
, it suffices to define the cases of
f(\delta(x))
and
f({x}\oplus_{p}{y})
,
the latter using
f(x)
and
f(y)
, in such a way that the convex algebra equations are respected. The recursion principle implies the following.

Proposition 3.3.

\mathcal{D}(A)
is the free convex algebra on
A
, in the sense that for any convex algebra
B
, and function

f:A\to B
, there exists a unique homomorphism of convex algebras
\overline{f}:\mathcal{D}(A)\to B
satisfying
f=\overline{f}\circ\delta
.
As a consequence,
\mathcal{D}(-)
forms a monad on the category of sets.

Now that we have a type
\mathcal{D}(A)
of probability distributions over a set
A
, we would like to reason about these distributions. In particular, we would like to know when two distributions are equal. For that, we use the following trick: we first relate probability distributions on finite sets to the classical probability distributions on these sets (that is, to functions into
[0,1]
with finite support). Then, we use functoriality of
\mathcal{D}
to generalise to arbitrary sets
A
.

Define the Bishop finite sets in the standard way by induction on
n
as
\mathsf{Fin}(0)=0
and
\mathsf{Fin}(n+1)=\mathsf{Fin}(n)+1
, where the
+
on
the right hand side refers to sum of types.
For these sets, we can relate
\mathcal{D}
to its classical definition.
First, we use that if any set
A
has decidable equality, we can associate a probability function
f_{\mu}:A\to[0,1]
to a distribution
\mu
by induction, using
f_{\delta(x)}(y)=1
if
x=y
and
f_{\delta(x)}(y)=0
else. This gives us an equivalence of types:

Lemma 3.4.

\mathcal{D}(\mathsf{Fin}(n))\simeq\Sigma(f:\mathsf{Fin}(n)\to[0,1]).\mathsf{sum}(f)=1

Here,
\mathsf{sum}
is the sum of the values of
f
, defined by induction on
n
.
Note that the right hand side of this equivalence indeed captures the classical definition of probability distributions over
\mathsf{Fin}(n)
.
We use Lemma
3.4
to reason about equality for probability distributions as follows.

Example 3.5.

The isomorphism of Lemma
3.4
allows us to prove equations of distributions
by first mapping to the right hand side of the isomorphism and then using functional extensionality. Consider, for example, the equation

\displaystyle{({a}\oplus_{p}{b})}\oplus_{p}{({c}\oplus_{p}{a})}={({b}\oplus_{\frac{1}{2}}{c})}\oplus_{2p(1-p)}{a},

where
a,b,c:\mathcal{D}(A)
for some
A
. In the case where
A=\mathsf{Fin}(3)
and
a=\delta(0),b=\delta(1),c=\delta(2)
,
we can prove this by noting that both the left and right-hand sides of the equation correspond to the map

f(0)=p^{2}+(1-p)^{2}=1+2p^{2}-2p=1-2p(1-p)
,
f(1)=f(2)=p(1-p)
.
Finally, the case of general
A,a,b,c
follows from applying functoriality to the canonical map
\mathsf{Fin}(3)\to\mathcal{D}A
.

Lastly, the guarded convex delay monad in the next section will be defined as a distribution on a sum type. To reason about such distributions, we will frequently use the fact that any distribution
\mu:\mathcal{D}(A+B)
on a sum type can be written uniquely as
a convex sum of two subdistributions: One on
A
and one on
B
. In the classical
setting where a distribution is a map with finite support this is trivial: The two subdistributions are simply the normalised restrictions
of the distribution map to
A
and
B
respectively. In the constructive type theoretic setting the proof requires a bit more work, so we mention this as a separate theorem.

Theorem 3.6.

For all sets
A
and
B
, the map

\mathcal{D}(A)+\mathcal{D}(B)+\mathcal{D}(A)\times(0,1)\times\mathcal{D}(B)\to\mathcal{D}(A+B),

defined by

\displaystyle f(\mathsf{in}_{1}(\mu))

\displaystyle\triangleq\mathcal{D}(\mathsf{inl})(\mu)

\displaystyle f(\mathsf{in}_{2}(\mu))

\displaystyle\triangleq\mathcal{D}(\mathsf{inr})(\mu)

\displaystyle f(\mathsf{in}_{3}(\mu,p,\nu))

\displaystyle\triangleq{(\mathcal{D}(\mathsf{inl})(\mu))}\oplus_{p}{(\mathcal{D}(\mathsf{inr})(\nu))},

is an equivalence of types.

4.
Convex delay algebras

In this section, we define the guarded convex delay monad
\mathsf{D}^{\kappa}
and the convex delay monad
\mathsf{D}^{\!\forall\!}
modelling the combination of probabilistic choice and recursion.
We first recall the notion of delay algebra (sometimes called a lifting or a
\triangleright^{\kappa}
-algebra
(
Paviotti et al., 2015
)
) and define a notion of convex delay algebra.

Definition 4.1 (Convex Delay Algebra).

A (
\kappa
-)
delay algebra
is a set
A
together with a map
{\mathsf{step}^{\kappa}:\triangleright^{\kappa}A\to A}
. A delay algebra
homomorphism is a map
f:A\to B
such that
f(\mathsf{step}^{\kappa}(a))=\mathsf{step}^{\kappa}(\char 12\relax{\alpha}\kappa{f(a\,[\alpha])})
for all
a:\triangleright^{\kappa}A
.
A
convex delay algebra
is a set
A
with both a delay algebra structure and a convex algebra structure, and a
homomorphism of these is a map respecting both structures.

Defining the functorial action of
\triangleright^{\kappa}
as
\triangleright^{\kappa}(f)(a)\triangleq\char 12\relax{\alpha}\kappa{f(a\,[\alpha])}
, the requirement for
f

being a delay algebra homomorphism can be expressed as the following commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\triangleright^{\kappa}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\triangleright^{\kappa}B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\triangleright^{\kappa}(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{step}^{\kappa}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{step}^{\kappa}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

Recall the guarded delay monad
{L^{\kappa}}
satisfying
{L^{\kappa}}A\simeq A+\triangleright^{\kappa}({L^{\kappa}}A)
. The type
{L^{\kappa}}A
is easily seen to be the free guarded delay algebra on a set
A
. Similarly, define the
guarded convex delay monad

as the guarded recursive type

\mathsf{D}^{\kappa}{A}\simeq\mathcal{D}(A+\triangleright^{\kappa}{(\mathsf{D}^{\kappa}{A})}).

Again, this can be constructed formally as a fixed point on a universe (assuming
A
lives in the same universe), but
we shall not spell this out here. We show that it is a free convex delay algebra. First
define the convex delay algebra structure (
\mathsf{step}^{\kappa},\oplus^{\kappa}
) on
\mathsf{D}^{\kappa}{A}
and the inclusion
\delta^{\kappa}:A\to\mathsf{D}^{\kappa}{A}

by

\displaystyle\mathsf{step}^{\kappa}(a)

\displaystyle=\delta(\mathsf{inr}(a))

\displaystyle{\mu}\oplus^{\kappa}_{p}{\nu}

\displaystyle={\mu}\oplus_{p}{\nu}

\displaystyle\delta^{\kappa}{a}

\displaystyle=\delta(\mathsf{inl}(a))

where the convex algebra structure on the right-hand sides of the equations above refers to those of
\mathcal{D}
.

Proposition 4.2.

\mathsf{D}^{\kappa}{A}
is the free convex delay algebra structure on
A
, for any set
A
. As a consequence
\mathsf{D}^{\kappa}{}

defines a monad on the category of sets.

Proof.

Suppose
f:A\to B
and that
B
is a convex delay algebra. We define the extension
\overline{f}:\mathsf{D}^{\kappa}{A}\to B

by guarded recursion, so suppose we are given
g:\triangleright^{\kappa}(\mathsf{D}^{\kappa}{A}\to B)
and define

\displaystyle\overline{f}(\mathsf{step}^{\kappa}(a))

\displaystyle=\mathsf{step}^{\kappa}(\char 12\relax\alpha\kappa{g\,[\alpha](a\,[\alpha])})

\displaystyle\overline{f}(\delta^{\kappa}(a))

\displaystyle=f(a)

\displaystyle\overline{f}({\mu}\oplus^{\kappa}_{p}{\nu})

\displaystyle={\overline{f}(\mu)}\oplus_{p}{\overline{f}(\nu)}.

Note that these cases define
\overline{f}
by induction on
\mathcal{D}
and
+
.
Unfolding the guarded recursive definition using (
4
) gives

\overline{f}(\mathsf{step}^{\kappa}(a))=\mathsf{step}^{\kappa}(\char 12\relax\alpha\kappa{\overline{f}(a\,[\alpha])}).

so that
\overline{f}
is a homomorphism of convex delay algebras. For uniqueness, suppose
g
is another
homomorphism extending
f
. We show that
g=\overline{f}
by guarded recursion and function extensionality.
So suppose we are given
p:\triangleright^{\kappa}(\Pi(x:\mathsf{D}^{\kappa}{A})(g(a)=\overline{f}(a)))
.
Then, for any
a:\triangleright^{\kappa}(\mathsf{D}^{\kappa}{A})
, the term
\char 12\relax\alpha\kappa{p\,[\alpha](a\,[\alpha])}
proves

{{\triangleright}\,(\alpha\!:\!\kappa).{(g(a\,[\alpha])=\overline{f}(a\,[\alpha]))}},

which by (
2
) is equivalent to

\char 12\relax\alpha\kappa{g(a\,[\alpha])}=\char 12\relax\alpha\kappa{\overline{f}(a\,[\alpha])}
.
Then,

\displaystyle g(\mathsf{step}^{\kappa}(a))

\displaystyle=\mathsf{step}^{\kappa}(\char 12\relax\alpha\kappa{g(a\,[\alpha])})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{step}^{\kappa}(\char 12\relax\alpha\kappa{\overline{f}(a\,[\alpha])})

\displaystyle=\overline{f}(\mathsf{step}^{\kappa}(a)).

The rest of the proof that
g(a)=\overline{f}(a)
, for all
a
, then follows by HIT induction on
\mathcal{D}
.

In terms of category theory, we have shown that
\mathsf{D}^{\kappa}
is left adjoint to the forgetful functor from
convex delay algebras to the category of sets. It therefore defines a monad on the category of sets.
∎

We will often write
t>\!>\!=^{\kappa}f
for
\overline{f}(t)
where
\overline{f}
is the unique extension of

f
to a convex delay algebra homomorphism.

Example 4.3.

The type
\mathsf{D}^{\kappa}A
can be thought of as a type of probabilistic processes returning values in
A
. Define, for example,
a geometric process with probability
p:(0,1)
as
\mathsf{geo}_{p}^{\kappa}\,0
where

\displaystyle\mathsf{geo}_{p}^{\kappa}

\displaystyle:\mathbb{N}\to\mathsf{D}^{\kappa}\mathbb{N}

\displaystyle\mathsf{geo}_{p}^{\kappa}\,n

\displaystyle\triangleq{(\delta^{\kappa}n)}\oplus_{p}{\mathsf{step}^{\kappa}(\char 12\relax\alpha\kappa{\mathsf{geo}_{p}^{\kappa}\,(n+1)})}

Note that this gives

\displaystyle\mathsf{geo}_{p}^{\kappa}(0)

\displaystyle={(\delta^{\kappa}0)}\oplus_{p}{}{\mathsf{step}^{\kappa}(\char 12\relax\alpha\kappa{\mathsf{geo}_{p}^{\kappa}\,(1)})}

\displaystyle={(\delta^{\kappa}0)}\oplus_{p}{\left(\mathsf{step}^{\kappa}\char 12\relax\alpha\kappa{\left({(\delta^{\kappa}1)}\oplus_{p}{(\mathsf{step}^{\kappa}\char 12\relax\alpha\kappa{\mathsf{geo}_{p}^{\kappa}\,(2)})}\right)}\right)}

\displaystyle=\ldots

The modal delay
\triangleright^{\kappa}
in the definition of
\mathsf{D}^{\kappa}A
prevents us from accessing values computed
later, thereby also preventing us from,
e.g.
, computing probabilities of ‘termination in
n
steps’ as elements of
[0,1]
. Such operations
should instead be defined on the convex delay monad
\mathsf{D}^{\!\forall\!}
. This monad is defined in terms of
\mathsf{D}^{\kappa}
by quantifying over clocks:

\mathsf{D}^{\!\forall\!}{A}\triangleq\forall\kappa.\mathsf{D}^{\kappa}A.

Proposition 4.4.

If
A
is clock-irrelevant then
\mathsf{D}^{\!\forall\!}{A}
is the final coalgebra
for the functor
F(X)=\mathcal{D}(A+X)
.

Proof.

We must show that
F
commutes with clock quantification, and this reduces easily to showing that

\mathcal{D}
commutes with clock quantification. The latter can be either proved directly by using the
same technique as for the similar result for the finite powerset functor
(
Kristensen et al., 2022
)
, or by referring to

(
Møgelberg and Zwart, 2023
, Proposition 14)
, which states that any free model monad for a theory with finite arities
commutes with clock quantification.
∎

Remark 1.

From now on, whenever we look at a type
\mathsf{D}^{\!\forall\!}{A}
, we assume
A
to be clock-irrelevant.

In particular,
\mathsf{D}^{\!\forall\!}{A}\simeq\mathcal{D}(A+\mathsf{D}^{\!\forall\!}A)
, and so
\mathsf{D}^{\!\forall\!}
carries a convex algebra structure
(\delta^{\forall\!}_{\,},\oplus^{\!\forall\!})

as well as a map
\mathsf{step}^{\!\forall\!}:\mathsf{D}^{\!\forall\!}A\to\mathsf{D}^{\!\forall\!}A
defined by
\mathsf{step}^{\!\forall\!}(x)=\delta(\mathsf{inr}(x))
. Define also
\delta^{\forall\!}:A\to\mathsf{D}^{\!\forall\!}{A}

as
\delta^{\forall\!}x\triangleq\delta(\mathsf{inl}\,x)
.

Example 4.5.

The geometric process can be defined as an element of
\mathbb{N}\to\mathsf{D}^{\!\forall\!}{\mathbb{N}}
as
\mathsf{geo}_{p}\,n\triangleq\Lambda\kappa.\mathsf{geo}_{p}^{\kappa}\,n
.
This satisfies the equations

\displaystyle\mathsf{geo}_{p}\,0

\displaystyle={(\delta^{\forall\!}\,0)}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{\mathsf{step}^{\!\forall\!}(\mathsf{geo}_{p}\,(1))}

\displaystyle={(\delta^{\forall\!}\,0)}\oplus_{p}{\mathsf{step}^{\!\forall\!}({(\delta^{\forall\!}\,1)}\oplus_{p}{\mathsf{step}^{\!\forall\!}(\mathsf{geo}_{p}\,(2))})}

\displaystyle=\dots

Lemma 4.6.

\mathsf{D}^{\!\forall\!}
carries a monad structure whose unit is
\delta^{\forall\!}
and where the Kleisli extension

\overline{f}:\mathsf{D}^{\!\forall\!}A\to\mathsf{D}^{\!\forall\!}B
of a map
f:A\to\mathsf{D}^{\!\forall\!}B
satisfies

\displaystyle\overline{f}(\mathsf{step}^{\!\forall\!}x)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{step}^{\!\forall\!}(\overline{f}(x))

\displaystyle\overline{f}({\mu}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{\nu})

\displaystyle={\overline{f}(\mu)}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{\overline{f}(\nu)}).

Proof.

This is a consequence of
(
Møgelberg and Zwart, 2023
, Lemma 16)
.
∎

4.1.
Probability of termination

Unfolding the type equivalence
\mathsf{D}^{\!\forall\!}{A}\simeq\mathcal{D}(A+\mathsf{D}^{\!\forall\!}A)
we can define maps out of
\mathsf{D}^{\!\forall\!}{A}
by cases. For example, we can define
the probability of immediate termination
\mathsf{PT}_{0}:\mathsf{D}^{\!\forall\!}A\to[0,1]
, as:

\displaystyle\mathsf{PT}_{0}(\delta^{\forall\!}a)

\displaystyle=1

\displaystyle\mathsf{PT}_{0}(\mathsf{step}^{\!\forall\!}d)

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\mathsf{PT}_{0}({x}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{y})

\displaystyle=p\cdot\mathsf{PT}_{0}(x)+(1-p)\cdot\mathsf{PT}_{0}(y).

Likewise, we define a function
\mathsf{run}:\mathsf{D}^{\!\forall\!}{A}\to\mathsf{D}^{\!\forall\!}{A}
that runs a computation for one step, eliminating a single level of
\mathsf{step}^{\!\forall\!}
operations:

\displaystyle\mathsf{run}(\delta^{\forall\!}a)

\displaystyle=\delta^{\forall\!}a

\displaystyle\mathsf{run}(\mathsf{step}^{\!\forall\!}d)

\displaystyle=d

\displaystyle\mathsf{run}({x}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{y})

\displaystyle={(\mathsf{run}\,x)}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{(\mathsf{run}\,y)}.

We can hence compute the probability of termination in
n
steps, by first running a computation for
n
steps, and then computing the
probability of immediate termination of the result:

\displaystyle\mathsf{PT}_{n}(x)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{PT}_{0}(\mathsf{run}^{n}x).

For example, running the geometric process for one step gives:

\displaystyle\mathsf{run}(\mathsf{geo}_{p}\,0)

\displaystyle={(\delta^{\forall\!}\,0)}\oplus_{p}{({(\delta^{\forall\!}\,1)}\oplus_{p}{\mathsf{step}^{\!\forall\!}(\mathsf{geo}_{p}\,(2))})},

so
\mathsf{PT}_{1}(\mathsf{geo}_{p}\,0)=p+(1-p)p=2p-p^{2}
.

We will later prove (Lemma
4.14
) that
\mathsf{PT}_{(.)}(\mu):\mathbb{N}\to[0,1]
is monotone for all
\mu
. Intuitively, the probability of
termination for
\mu
is the limit of this sequence. The following definition gives a simple way of comparing such limits, without introducing real
numbers to our type theory.

Definition 4.7.

Let
f,g\colon\mathbb{N}\to[0,1]
be monotone. Write
f\leq_{\mathrm{lim}}g
for the following proposition:
for every
n\colon\mathbb{N}
and
every rational
\varepsilon>0
there exists
m\colon\mathbb{N}
such that

f(n)\leq g(m)+\epsilon
. We write
f=_{\mathrm{lim}}g
for the conjunction
f\leq_{\mathrm{lim}}g
and
g\leq_{\mathrm{lim}}f
.

It is easy to show that the relation
\leq_{\mathrm{lim}}
is reflexive and transitive and thus
=_{\mathrm{lim}}
is an equivalence relation.
Furthermore,
\leq_{\mathrm{lim}}
is closed under pointwise convex sums.

Lemma 4.8.

Let
f_{1},g_{1},f_{2},g_{2}\colon\mathbb{N}\to[0,1]
be monotone and let
p\in(0,1)
.
If
f_{1}\leq_{\mathrm{lim}}g_{1}
and
f_{2}\leq_{\mathrm{lim}}g_{2}
, then
p\cdot f_{1}+(1-p)\cdot f_{2}\leq_{\mathrm{lim}}p\cdot g_{1}+(1-p)\cdot g_{2}
.

4.2.
Step reductions

Rather than eliminating a full level of
\mathsf{step}^{\!\forall\!}
operations with
\mathsf{run}
, it is sometimes useful to allow different branches to run for a different number of steps. We capture this in the
step reduction relation

\leadsto
. This is the reflexive, transitive, and convex closure of the one-step reduction relation:

Definition 4.9.

Define the step reduction relation
\leadsto:\mathsf{D}^{\!\forall\!}A\to\mathsf{D}^{\!\forall\!}A\to\mathsf{Prop}
inductively by the following rules

\frac{}{\nu\leadsto\nu}\frac{}{\mathsf{step}^{\!\forall\!}\nu\leadsto\nu}\frac{\nu\leadsto\nu^{\prime}\nu^{\prime}\leadsto\nu^{\prime\prime}}{\nu\leadsto\nu^{\prime\prime}}\frac{\nu_{1}\leadsto\nu_{1}^{\prime}\nu_{2}\leadsto\nu_{2}^{\prime}}{{\nu_{1}}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{\nu_{2}}\leadsto{\nu_{1}^{\prime}}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{\nu_{2}^{\prime}}}

Remark 2.

CCTT lacks higher inductive families as primitive, but the
special case of the step reduction relation can be represented by defining
a fuelled version
\leadsto^{n}
by induction on
n
, and existentially quantifying
n
.

The step reduction relation
\leadsto
is closely related to
\mathsf{run}
.

Lemma 4.10.

Let
\nu,\nu^{\prime}:\mathsf{D}^{\!\forall\!}A
. Then:

(1)

For all
n:\mathbb{N}
,
\nu\leadsto\mathsf{run}^{n}\nu
.

(2)

If
\nu\leadsto\nu^{\prime}
then, for all
n:\mathbb{N}
,
\mathsf{run}^{n}\nu\leadsto\mathsf{run}^{n}\nu^{\prime}
.

(3)

If
\nu\leadsto\nu^{\prime}
then there exists an
n:\mathbb{N}
such that
\nu^{\prime}\leadsto\mathsf{run}^{n}\nu.

Proof.

The first two facts are proven by induction on
n
, the last by induction on
\leadsto
.
∎

Unlike
\mathsf{run}
, the relation
\leadsto
can execute different branches of a probabilistic computation for a different number of steps, as illustrated in the following example.

Example 4.11.

Consider:

\nu={(\mathsf{step}^{\!\forall\!}(\delta^{\forall\!}a))}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{(\mathsf{step}^{\!\forall\!}(\mathsf{step}^{\!\forall\!}(\delta^{\forall\!}b)))}
.
Then
\mathsf{run}
removes
\mathsf{step}^{\!\forall\!}
operations from both branches.

\displaystyle\mathsf{run}(\nu)

\displaystyle={(\delta^{\forall\!}a)}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{(\mathsf{step}^{\!\forall\!}(\delta^{\forall\!}b))}

\displaystyle\mathsf{run}^{2}(\nu)

\displaystyle={(\delta^{\forall\!}a)}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{(\delta^{\forall\!}b)}

\displaystyle\mathsf{run}^{n}(\nu)

\displaystyle={(\delta^{\forall\!}a)}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{(\delta^{\forall\!}b)}\text{ for }n\geq 2.

As per Lemma
4.10
, we have
\nu\leadsto(\mathsf{run}^{n}\nu)
, for each
n:\mathbb{N}
, as well as:

\displaystyle\nu

\displaystyle\leadsto{(\delta^{\forall\!}a)}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{(\mathsf{step}^{\!\forall\!}(\mathsf{step}^{\!\forall\!}(\delta^{\forall\!}b)))}

\displaystyle\nu

\displaystyle\leadsto{(\mathsf{step}^{\!\forall\!}(\delta^{\forall\!}a))}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{(\mathsf{step}^{\!\forall\!}(\delta^{\forall\!}b))}

\displaystyle\nu

\displaystyle\leadsto{(\mathsf{step}^{\!\forall\!}(\delta^{\forall\!}a))}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{(\delta^{\forall\!}b)}.

We will use this flexibility in running different branches for a different number of steps in both our proofs and examples, such as in the proof of Lemma
7.9
.

The step reduction relation is confluent, since we can always match two probabilistic processes that stem from the same parent process by running all branches long enough.

Corollary 4.12 (Confluence).

Let
\nu,\nu_{1},\nu_{2}:\mathsf{D}^{\!\forall\!}A
.

If both
\nu\leadsto\nu_{1}
and
\nu\leadsto\nu_{2}
,

then there is a
\nu^{\prime}:\mathsf{D}^{\!\forall\!}
such that
\nu_{1}\leadsto\nu^{\prime}
and
\nu_{2}\leadsto\nu^{\prime}
.

v
v_{1}
v_{2}
v^{\prime}

Proof.

We apply Lemma
4.10
.
3
, giving us an
n_{1}
and
n_{2}
such that
\nu_{1}\leadsto\mathsf{run}^{n_{1}}\nu
and
\nu_{2}\leadsto\mathsf{run}^{n_{2}}\nu
. Taking
N=\max(n_{1},n_{2})
then gives the confluence via Lemma
4.10
.
1
.
∎

We mention two more useful properties of step reductions. Firstly, the bind operation preserves the
\leadsto
relation, which follows by an easy induction on
\leadsto
.

Lemma 4.13.

If
f:A\rightarrow\mathsf{D}^{\!\forall\!}(B)
, and
\nu,\nu^{\prime}:\mathsf{D}^{\!\forall\!}A
such that
\nu\leadsto\nu^{\prime}
. Then also

\overline{f}(\nu)\leadsto\overline{f}(\nu^{\prime})
.

Secondly, the probability of termination is monotone with respect to
n
, and along step reductions:

Lemma 4.14.

For
\nu,\nu^{\prime}:\mathsf{D}^{\!\forall\!}A
, we have:

(1)

For all
n,m:\mathbb{N}
such that
n\leq m
:
\mathsf{PT}_{n}(\nu)\leq\mathsf{PT}_{m}(\nu)
.

(2)

If
\nu\leadsto\nu^{\prime}
then for all
n:\mathbb{N}
:
\mathsf{PT}_{n}(\nu)\leq\mathsf{PT}_{n}(\nu^{\prime})
.

(3)

If
\nu\leadsto\nu^{\prime}
then
\mathsf{PT}_{(.)}(\nu)=_{\mathrm{lim}}\mathsf{PT}_{(.)}(\nu^{\prime})
.

Proof.

The first statement is by induction on
n
and case analysis for
\nu
, the second statement by induction on
n
and
\leadsto
.
The third statement follows by induction on
\leadsto
, the properties of
\leq_{\mathrm{lim}}
and monotonicity of
\mathsf{PT}_{(.)}(-)
(i.e. the first statement of this lemma).
∎

4.3.
Approximate step reductions

The monads
\mathsf{D}^{\kappa}
and
\mathsf{D}^{\!\forall\!}
model the fact that at any point in time we might not have complete information about a probability distribution, with more information coming in at every time
step. The relations
\mathsf{run}
and
\leadsto
process the incoming information and give new probability distributions that are future variants of the original (delayed) distribution, after a finite number of
unfoldings. However, it is often useful to talk about what happens in the limit, after infinitely many unfoldings. For this purpose we introduce the relation
\leadsto^{\approx}
. Intuitively,
v\leadsto^{\approx}v^{\prime}

holds if we can get arbitrarily close to
v^{\prime}
by applying sufficiently many unfoldings to
v
.

Definition 4.15.

Define the
approximate step reduction
relation
\leadsto^{\approx}:\mathsf{D}^{\!\forall\!}A\to\mathsf{D}^{\!\forall\!}A\to\mathsf{Prop}
as:

\nu\leadsto^{\approx}\nu^{\prime}
if for all
p\in(0,1)
there is a
\nu^{\prime\prime}:\mathsf{D}^{\!\forall\!}A
such that
\nu\leadsto{\nu^{\prime}}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{\nu^{\prime\prime}}
.

Example 4.16.

The approximate step reduction relation
\leadsto^{\approx}
allows us to talk about limits constructively. For example, say
h_{x}
is a hesitant point distribution
h_{x}
, if

h_{x}\leadsto{\delta^{\forall\!}(x)}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{q}{h_{x}}
. For instance,
h_{x}=\Lambda\kappa.(\mathsf{fix}^{\kappa}{(}\lambda y.{\delta^{\kappa}(x)}\oplus^{\kappa}_{q}{\mathsf{step}^{\kappa}{y}}))
satisfies this, but we will see another example in section
9.1
. In the limit,
h_{x}
should be the same distribution as the normal point distribution
\delta^{\forall\!}(x)
.
Indeed,

h_{x}\leadsto^{\approx}\delta^{\forall\!}(x).

To prove this, we need to show that for all
p\in(0,1)
there is a
\nu
such that
h_{x}\leadsto{\delta^{\forall\!}(x)}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{\nu}
.
Let, for any natural number
n
,
q_{n}=\sum_{i=0}^{n}q(1-q)^{i}
, then by applying transitivity of
\leadsto

n
times:

h_{x}\leadsto{\delta^{\forall\!}(x)}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{q_{n}}{h_{x}}.

If we choose
n
such that
q_{n}\geq p
, then we can write

{\delta^{\forall\!}(x)}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{q_{n}}{h_{x}}={\delta^{\forall\!}(x)}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{({\delta^{\forall\!}(x)}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{\frac{q_{n}-p}{1-p}}{h_{x}})}.

Then choosing
\nu={\delta^{\forall\!}(x)}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{\frac{q_{n}-p}{1-p}}{h_{x}}
gives
h_{x}\leadsto{\delta^{\forall\!}(x)}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{\nu}
, which is what we needed to show.

We again prove some useful properties of
\leadsto^{\approx}
that we need in future proofs.
We start with the defining properties of the step reductions. These also hold for approximate step reductions:

Lemma 4.17.

The relation
\leadsto^{\approx}
is reflexive and transitive. Furthermore,
\mathsf{step}^{\!\forall\!}\nu\leadsto^{\approx}\nu
, and
\leadsto^{\approx}
is preserved by sums: if
\nu_{1}\leadsto^{\approx}\nu_{1}^{\prime}
and
\nu_{2}\leadsto^{\approx}\nu_{2}^{\prime}
, then for all
p\in(0,1),{\nu_{1}}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{\nu_{2}}\leadsto^{\approx}{\nu_{1}^{\prime}}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{\nu_{2}^{\prime}}
.

The bind operation also preserves approximate step reductions, which follows directly from the fact that the bind operation preserves
\leadsto
.

Lemma 4.18.

If
f:A\rightarrow\mathsf{D}^{\!\forall\!}(B)
, and
\nu,\nu^{\prime}:\mathsf{D}^{\!\forall\!}A
such that
\nu\leadsto^{\approx}\nu^{\prime}
. Then also

\overline{f}(\nu)\leadsto^{\approx}\overline{f}(\nu^{\prime})
.

Step reductions are also approximate step reductions, and approximately reducing step reductions just results in further approximate reductions, as does step reducing an approximate step reduction.

Lemma 4.19.

Let
\nu,\nu_{1},\nu_{2}:\mathsf{D}^{\!\forall\!}{A}
. Then:

(1)

If
\nu\leadsto\nu_{1}
, then
\nu\leadsto^{\approx}\nu_{1}
.

(2)

If
\nu\leadsto\nu_{1}
and
\nu_{1}\leadsto^{\approx}\nu_{2}
, then
\nu\leadsto^{\approx}\nu_{2}
.

(3)

If
\nu\leadsto^{\approx}\nu_{1}
and
\nu_{1}\leadsto\nu_{2}
, then
\nu\leadsto^{\approx}\nu_{2}
.

Finally we show that approximate step reductions preserve limit probabilities of termination:

Lemma 4.20.

Let
\nu_{1},\nu_{2}:\mathsf{D}^{\!\forall\!}{A}
. If
\nu_{1}\leadsto^{\approx}\nu_{2}
then
\mathsf{PT}_{(.)}(\nu_{1})=_{\mathrm{lim}}\mathsf{PT}_{(.)}(\nu_{2})
.

5.
Probabilistic FPC

In this section we define the language
\mathsf{FPC}_{\oplus}
, its typing rules, and operational semantics in
CCTT.
\mathsf{FPC}_{\oplus}
is the extension of simply typed lambda calculus with recursive types and probabilistic choice.
The typing rules of
\mathsf{FPC}_{\oplus}
are presented in Figure
2
. In typing judgements,
\Gamma
is assumed to be
a variable context and all types (which include recursive types of the form
\mu{X}.{\tau}
) are closed.

\frac{x:\sigma\in\Gamma\vdash\Gamma}{\Gamma\vdash x:\sigma}\frac{\vdash\Gamma}{\Gamma\vdash\langle\rangle:1}\frac{n:\mathbb{N}\vdash\Gamma}{\Gamma\vdash\underline{n}:\mathsf{Nat}}\frac{\Gamma\vdash L:\mathsf{Nat}\Gamma\vdash M:\sigma\Gamma\vdash N:\sigma}{\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{ifz}(L,M,N):\sigma}\frac{\Gamma\vdash M:\mathsf{Nat}}{\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{suc}{\,M}:\mathsf{Nat}}\frac{\Gamma\vdash M:\sigma}{\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{inl}{\,M}:{\sigma}+{\tau}}\frac{\Gamma\vdash L:{\sigma}+{\sigma^{\prime}}\Gamma,x:\sigma\vdash M:\tau\Gamma,y:\sigma^{\prime}\vdash N:\tau}{\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{case}(L,x.M,y.N):\tau}\frac{\Gamma\vdash M:\sigma\Gamma\vdash N:\tau}{\Gamma\vdash\langle{M},{N}\rangle:{\sigma}\times{\tau}}\frac{\Gamma\vdash M:{\sigma}\times{\tau}}{\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{fst}{\,M}:\sigma}\frac{\Gamma,(x:\sigma)\vdash M:\tau}{\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{lam}\ {x}.{M}:\sigma\to\tau}\frac{\Gamma\vdash N:\sigma\to\tau\Gamma\vdash M:\sigma}{\Gamma\vdash NM:\tau}\frac{\Gamma\vdash M:\tau[\mu{X}.{\tau}/X]}{\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{fold}{\,M}:\mu{X}.{\tau}}\frac{\Gamma\vdash M:\mu{X}.{\tau}}{\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{unfold}{\,M}:\tau[\mu{X}.{\tau}/X]}\frac{\Gamma\vdash M:\sigma\Gamma\vdash N:\sigma p:(0,1)}{\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{choice}^{p}(M,N):\sigma}

Figure 2.
Typing rules for
\mathsf{FPC}_{\oplus}
. Rules for
\mathsf{pred}\,{},\mathsf{inr}{\,},\mathsf{snd}{\,}
omitted.

We assume a given representation of terms and types of
\mathsf{FPC}_{\oplus}
within CCTT. For example, as inductive types with the notion of closedness defined as decidable properties
on these types. We will also assume that terms are annotated with enough types so that one can deduce the type
of subterms from terms. This allows the evaluation function and the denotational semantics to be defined by
induction on terms rather than typing judgement derivations. It also means the typing judgement

\Gamma\vdash M:\sigma
is decidable. We write
Ty
for the type of closed types, and
\mathsf{Tm}^{\Gamma}_{\mathsf{\sigma}}

for the type of terms
M
satisfying
\Gamma\vdash M:\sigma
, constructed as a subtype of the type of all terms.
For empty
\Gamma
we write simply
\mathsf{Tm}_{\mathsf{\sigma}}
. We write

\mathsf{Val}_{\mathsf{\sigma}}
for the set of closed values of type
\sigma
, as captured by the grammar

\displaystyle V,W

\displaystyle:=\langle\rangle\mid\underline{n}\mid\mathsf{lam}\ {x}.{M}\mid\mathsf{fold}{\,V}\mid\langle{V},{W}\rangle\mid\mathsf{inl}{\,V}\mid\mathsf{inr}{\,V}

The
operational semantics
is given by a function
\mathsf{eval}^{\kappa}
of type

(6)

\mathsf{eval}^{\kappa}:\{\sigma:\textsf{Ty}\}\to\mathsf{Tm}_{\mathsf{\sigma}}\to\mathsf{D}^{\kappa}(\mathsf{Val}_{\mathsf{\sigma}}),

associating to a term
M
with type
\sigma
an element of the free guarded convex delay algebra over
\mathsf{Val}_{\mathsf{\sigma}}
.
The corresponding element of the coinductive convex delay algebra
can be defined as

\mathsf{eval}(M)\triangleq\Lambda\kappa.\mathsf{eval}^{\kappa}(M):\mathsf{D}^{\!\forall\!}{}(\mathsf{Val}_{\mathsf{\sigma}}).

The function
\mathsf{eval}^{\kappa}
is defined by an outer guarded recursion and an inner
induction on terms. In other words, by first assuming given a term of type

\triangleright^{\kappa}(\{\sigma:\textsf{Ty}\}\to\mathsf{Tm}_{\mathsf{\sigma}}\to\mathsf{D}^{\kappa}(\mathsf{Val}_{\mathsf{\sigma}}))
, then defining
\mathsf{eval}^{\kappa}(M)

by induction on
M
.
The cases are given in Figure
3
. The figure
overloads names of some term constructors to functions of values, e.g.

{{\smash{\mathbf{inl}}}}:\mathsf{Val}_{\mathsf{\sigma}}\to\mathsf{Val}_{\mathsf{\sigma+\tau}}
mapping
V
to
\mathsf{inl}{\,V}
, and
{{\smash{\mathbf{pred}}}},{{\smash{\mathbf{suc}}}}:\mathsf{Val}_{\mathsf{\mathsf{Nat}}}\to\mathsf{Val}_{\mathsf{\mathsf{Nat}}}

defined using
\mathsf{Val}_{\mathsf{\mathsf{Nat}}}\simeq\mathbb{N}
. The figure also uses pattern matching within bindings,
e.g. in the case of function application, where we use that all values of function type are of the form
\mathsf{lam}\ {x}.{M^{\prime}}
for some
M^{\prime}
.

In the cases of
\mathsf{case}(L,x.M,y.N)
and
M\,N
the recursive calls
to
\mathsf{eval}^{\kappa}
are under
\mathsf{step}^{\kappa}
and so can be justified by guarded recursion. This is necessary,
because they are not instantiated at a structurally smaller term. The case
for
\mathsf{unfold}{\,M}
also introduces a computation step using
\mathsf{step}^{\kappa}
. While
this is not strictly necessary to define the operational
semantics, we introduce it to simulate the steps of the denotational
semantics defined in Section
6
. This simplifies the proof of Theorem
6.1
.

\displaystyle\mathsf{eval}^{\kappa}(V)

\displaystyle\triangleq\delta^{\kappa}V

\displaystyle\mathsf{eval}^{\kappa}(\mathsf{suc}{\,M})

\displaystyle\triangleq\mathsf{D}^{\kappa}({{\smash{\mathbf{suc}}}})(\mathsf{eval}^{\kappa}M)

\displaystyle\mathsf{eval}^{\kappa}(\mathsf{inl}{\,M})

\displaystyle\triangleq\mathsf{D}^{\kappa}({{\smash{\mathbf{inl}}}})(\mathsf{eval}^{\kappa}M)

\displaystyle\mathsf{eval}^{\kappa}(\mathsf{ifz}(L,M,N))

\displaystyle\triangleq\mathsf{eval}^{\kappa}{L}>\!>\!=^{\kappa}\begin{cases}{\underline{0}}\mapsto{\mathsf{eval}^{\kappa}(M)}\\
{\underline{n+1}}\mapsto{\mathsf{eval}^{\kappa}(N)}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\mathsf{eval}^{\kappa}(\mathsf{fst}{\,M})

\displaystyle\triangleq(\mathsf{eval}^{\kappa}M)>\!>\!=^{\kappa}\lambda\langle{V},{W}\rangle.\delta^{\kappa}V

\displaystyle\mathsf{eval}^{\kappa}{\langle{M},{N}\rangle}

\displaystyle\triangleq\mathsf{eval}^{\kappa}{M}>\!>\!=^{\kappa}\lambda V.\mathsf{eval}^{\kappa}{N}>\!>\!=^{\kappa}\lambda W.\delta^{\kappa}{\langle{V},{W}\rangle}

\displaystyle\mathsf{eval}^{\kappa}(\mathsf{case}(L,x.M,y.N))

\displaystyle\triangleq\mathsf{eval}^{\kappa}(L)>\!>\!=^{\kappa}\begin{cases}{\mathsf{inl}{\,V}}\mapsto{\mathsf{step}^{\kappa}{}(\char 12\relax\alpha\kappa\mathsf{eval}^{\kappa}(M[V/x]))}\\
{\mathsf{inr}{\,V}}\mapsto{\mathsf{step}^{\kappa}{}(\char 12\relax\alpha\kappa\mathsf{eval}^{\kappa}(N[V/y]))}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\mathsf{eval}^{\kappa}(M\,N)

\displaystyle\triangleq\mathsf{eval}^{\kappa}(M)>\!>\!=^{\kappa}\lambda(\mathsf{lam}\ {x}.{M^{\prime}}).\ \mathsf{eval}^{\kappa}(N)

\displaystyle\phantom{\triangleq\triangleq}>\!>\!=^{\kappa}\lambda V.\mathsf{step}^{\kappa}{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\char 12\relax\alpha\kappa\mathsf{eval}^{\kappa}(M^{\prime}[V/x])$}\mathclose{}{)}}}

\displaystyle\mathsf{eval}^{\kappa}(\mathsf{fold}{\,M})

\displaystyle\triangleq\mathsf{D}^{\kappa}(\mathsf{fold}{\,)}(\mathsf{eval}^{\kappa}(M))

\displaystyle\mathsf{eval}^{\kappa}(\mathsf{unfold}{\,M})

\displaystyle\triangleq\mathsf{eval}^{\kappa}M>\!>\!=^{\kappa}\lambda(\mathsf{fold}{\,V}).\mathsf{step}^{\kappa}(\char 12\relax\alpha\kappa{\delta^{\kappa}V)}

\displaystyle\mathsf{eval}^{\kappa}{(\mathsf{choice}^{p}(M,N))}

\displaystyle\triangleq{(\mathsf{eval}^{\kappa}{M})}\oplus^{\kappa}_{p}{(\mathsf{eval}^{\kappa}N)}

Figure 3.
The evaluation function
\mathsf{eval}^{\kappa}{}
. Rules for
\mathsf{pred}\,{},\mathsf{inr}{\,},\mathsf{snd}{\,}
omitted.

In
\mathsf{FPC}_{\oplus}
we can define a fixpoint combinator as illustrated below:

Example 5.1.

We define
\cdot\vdash\mathsf{Y}:{({({\sigma}\to{\tau})}\to{({\sigma}\to{\tau})})}\to{{\sigma}\to{\tau}}
by

\mathsf{Y}\triangleq\ \mathsf{lam}\ {f}.{\mathsf{lam}\ {z}.{e_{f}(\mathsf{fold}{\,e_{f}})z}}
, where

\displaystyle e_{f}

\displaystyle:{(\mu{X}.{({X}\to{{\sigma}\to{\tau}})})}\to{{\sigma}\to{\tau}}

\displaystyle e_{f}

\displaystyle\triangleq\mathsf{lam}\ {y}.{\mathrm{let}\ {y^{\prime}}={\mathsf{unfold}{\,y}}\ \mathrm{in}\ f(\mathsf{lam}\ {x}.{y^{\prime}yx})}

Here,
y:\mu{X}.{({X}\to{{\sigma}\to{\tau}})}
,

y^{\prime}:{(\mu{X}.{({X}\to{{\sigma}\to{\tau}})})}\to{{\sigma}\to{\tau}}

and
\mathsf{lam}\ {x}.{y^{\prime}yx}:{\sigma}\to{\tau}
.

Then, for any values
\cdot\vdash f:{({\sigma}\to{\tau})}\to{{\sigma}\to{\tau}}
and
\cdot\vdash V:\sigma
,

\mathsf{eval}^{\kappa}{((\mathsf{Y}f)(V))}=(\Delta^{\kappa})^{4}(\mathsf{eval}^{\kappa}{((f(\mathsf{Y}f))(V))})

where
\Delta^{\kappa}\triangleq(\mathsf{step}^{\kappa}\circ\mathsf{next}^{\kappa})
.

5.1.
Contextual Refinement

Two terms
M,N
are contextually equivalent if for any closing context
C[-]
of ground type
the terms
C[M]
and
C[N]
have the same observational behaviour. Here,
the only observable behaviour we consider is the probability of termination for programs of type
1
, which, for a
closed term
M
should be the limit of the sequence
\mathsf{PT}_{n}(\mathsf{eval}\,M)
.

Definition 5.2 (Contextual Refinement).

Let
\Gamma\vdash M,N:\tau
be terms. We say that
M

contextually refines

N

if for any closing context of unit type
C:({\Gamma}\vdash{\sigma})\Rightarrow({\cdot}\vdash{1})
,

\mathsf{PT}_{(.)}(\mathsf{eval}(C[M]))\leq_{\mathrm{lim}}\mathsf{PT}_{(.)}(\mathsf{eval}(C[N]))
.
In this case, write
M\preceq_{\mathsf{Ctx}}N
. We say that
M
and
N
are contextually equivalent (
M\equiv_{\mathsf{Ctx}}N
) if

M\preceq_{\mathsf{Ctx}}N
and
N\preceq_{\mathsf{Ctx}}M
.

Definition
5.2
should be read as defining a predicate on
M
and
N
in CCTT,
i.e.
, a
function
\mathsf{Tm}^{\Gamma}_{\mathsf{\sigma}}\to\mathsf{Tm}^{\Gamma}_{\mathsf{\sigma}}\to\mathsf{Prop}
.
The notion of closing context
C:({\Gamma}\vdash{\sigma})\Rightarrow({\cdot}\vdash{1})

is a special case of a typing judgement on contexts
C:({\Gamma}\vdash{\sigma})\Rightarrow({\Delta}\vdash{\tau})
defined in a standard way.
Note that
C[-]
may capture free variables in
M
.

6.
Denotational semantics

Interpretation of types

\displaystyle\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle{\mathsf{Nat}}$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}

\displaystyle\triangleq\mathbb{N}

\displaystyle\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle{{\sigma}\times{\tau}}$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}

\displaystyle\triangleq\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle{\sigma}$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}\times\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle{\tau}$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}

\displaystyle\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle{{\sigma}\to{\tau}}$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}

\displaystyle\triangleq\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle{\sigma}$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}\to\mathsf{D}^{\kappa}{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathsf{\tau}$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}}

\displaystyle\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle{1}$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}

\displaystyle\triangleq\mathsf{1}

\displaystyle\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle{{\sigma}+{\tau}}$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}

\displaystyle\triangleq\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle{\sigma}$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}+\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle{\tau}$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}

\displaystyle\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle{\mu{X}.{\tau}}$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}

\displaystyle\triangleq\triangleright^{\kappa}{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle{\tau[\mu{X}.{\tau}/X]}$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}}

Interpretation of terms

\displaystyle\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\langle\rangle$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}_{\rho}\triangleq\delta^{\kappa}(\star)\quad\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\underline{n}$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}_{\rho}

\displaystyle\triangleq\delta^{\kappa}(n)\quad\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}_{\rho}\triangleq\delta^{\kappa}(\rho(x))

\displaystyle\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathsf{suc}{\,M}$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}_{\rho}

\displaystyle\triangleq\mathsf{D}^{\kappa}{({{\smash{\mathbf{suc}}}}{})}\left(\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\Gamma\vdash M:\mathsf{Nat}$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}_{\rho}\right)

\displaystyle\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathsf{ifz}(t,M,N)$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}_{\rho}

\displaystyle\triangleq\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle L$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}_{\rho}>\!>\!=^{\kappa}\begin{cases}0&\mapsto{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle M$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}_{\rho}}\\
n+1&\mapsto{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle N$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}_{\rho}}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathsf{lam}\ {x}.{M}$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}_{\rho}

\displaystyle\triangleq\delta^{\kappa}\left(\lambda(v:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\sigma$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\mathsf{Val},\kappa}_{\rho}).\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle M$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}_{\rho.x\mapsto v}\right)

\displaystyle\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle M\,N$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}_{\rho}

\displaystyle\triangleq\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle M$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}_{\rho}>\!>\!=^{\kappa}\lambda f.\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle N$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}_{\rho}>\!>\!=^{\kappa}\lambda v.fv

\displaystyle\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\langle{M},{N}\rangle$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}_{\rho}

\displaystyle\triangleq\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle M$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}_{\rho}>\!>\!=^{\kappa}\lambda v.\left(\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle N$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}_{\rho}>\!>\!=^{\kappa}\lambda w.\delta^{\kappa}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle{v},{w}$}\mathclose{}{)}}\right)

\displaystyle\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathsf{fst}{\,M}$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}_{\rho}

\displaystyle\triangleq\mathsf{D}^{\kappa}{(\mathsf{pr}_{1}{})}\left(\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle M$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}_{\rho}\right)

\displaystyle\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathsf{inl}{\,M}$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}_{\rho}

\displaystyle\triangleq\mathsf{D}^{\kappa}{({{\smash{\mathbf{inl}}}}\,{})}\left(\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle M$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}_{\rho}\right)

\displaystyle\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathsf{case}(L,x.M,y.N)$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}_{\rho}

\displaystyle\triangleq\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle L$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}_{\rho}>\!>\!=^{\kappa}\begin{cases}{{\smash{\mathbf{inl}}}}\,{v}\mapsto{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle M$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}_{\rho.x\mapsto v}}\\
{{\smash{\mathbf{inr}}}}\,{v}\mapsto{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle N$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}_{\rho.y\mapsto v}}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathsf{fold}{\,M}$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}_{\rho}

\displaystyle\triangleq\mathsf{D}^{\kappa}{(\mathsf{next}^{\kappa})}(\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle M$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}_{\rho})

\displaystyle\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathsf{unfold}{\,M}$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}_{\rho}

\displaystyle\triangleq\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle M$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}_{\rho}>\!>\!=^{\kappa}\lambda v.\mathsf{step}^{\kappa}(\char 12\relax\alpha\kappa\delta^{\kappa}{(v\,[\alpha])})

\displaystyle\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathsf{choice}^{p}(M,N)$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}_{\rho}

\displaystyle\triangleq{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle M$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}_{\rho}}\oplus^{\kappa}_{p}{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle N$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}_{\rho}}

Figure 4.
Denotational semantics for
\mathsf{FPC}_{\oplus}
. Rules for
\mathsf{pred}\,{},\mathsf{inr}{\,},\mathsf{snd}{\,}
omitted.

We now define a
denotational semantics
for
\mathsf{FPC}_{\oplus}
.
The semantics is based on two ideas: (i) we use guarded recursion to interpret recursive types,
inspired by
(
Møgelberg and Paviotti, 2019
)
, and (ii) we use the guarded convex delay monad
to interpret effectful computations, similarly to how monads are used to interpret effectful languages in standard denotational semantics.
Specifically we define two functions

\displaystyle\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle{-}$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}

\displaystyle:\textsf{Ty}\to\mathsf{Set}

\displaystyle\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle-$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}_{-}

\displaystyle:\{\Gamma:\textsf{Ctx}\}\to\{\sigma:\textsf{Ty}\}\to\mathsf{Tm}^{\Gamma}_{\mathsf{\sigma}}\to\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle{\Gamma}$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}\to\mathsf{D}^{\kappa}{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathsf{\sigma}$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}}

which interpret types and terms, respectively, see Figure
4
. Note that by using guarded recursion to interpret types,
it suffices to define the denotation of closed types.

The interpretation of terms is defined by induction on terms.
Note that all syntactic values are interpreted
as semantic values in the sense that we can define a map

\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle-$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\mathsf{Val},\kappa}:\{\sigma:\textsf{Ty}\}\to\mathsf{Val}_{\mathsf{\sigma}}\to\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle{\sigma}$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}

satisfying
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle V$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}=\delta^{\kappa}(\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle V$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\mathsf{Val},\kappa})
for all
V
. Note also that
the denotational semantics only steps when unfolding from a recursive type. As a consequence,
the denotational semantics is much less cluttered by steps than the operational semantics,
which makes it simpler to use for reasoning. Despite the fewer steps, the two semantics
refine each other. In particular we have the theorem below, which we will prove using
the techniques we develop in later sections:

Theorem 6.1.

For any well-typed closed expression
\cdot\vdash M:1
we have that

\mathsf{PT}_{(.)}(\mathsf{eval}\,M)=_{\mathrm{lim}}\mathsf{PT}_{(.)}(\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle M$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}})

where
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle M$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}\triangleq\Lambda\kappa.\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle M$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}

The denotational semantics satisfies the following substitution lemma:

Lemma 6.2 (Substitution Lemma).

For any well-typed term
\Gamma.(x:\sigma)\vdash M:\tau
as well as every well typed value
\Gamma\vdash V:\sigma
we have

\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle M[V/x]$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}_{\rho}=\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle M$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}_{\rho.x\mapsto\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle V$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\mathsf{Val},\kappa}_{\rho}}

The following example presents a useful equality to work with denotational semantics of recursive
functions defined via a fixed point combinator:

Example 6.3.

Recall the fixed point operator
\mathsf{Y}
from Example
5.1
. The denotational semantics
satisfies the following equations

(7)

\displaystyle\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle(\mathsf{lam}\ {y}.{M})\,x$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}_{x\mapsto v}

\displaystyle=\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle M$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}_{y\mapsto v}

(8)

\displaystyle\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathsf{Y}f\,x$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}_{x\mapsto v}

\displaystyle=(\mathsf{step}^{\kappa}{}\circ\mathsf{next}^{\kappa})(\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle f(\mathsf{Y}f)\,x$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}_{x\mapsto v})

for any value
f
and semantic value
v
, and where
x
does not appear free in
M
. By applying the substitution lemma, we also get

\displaystyle\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle(\mathsf{lam}\ {y}.{M})\,V$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}

\displaystyle=\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle M[V/x]$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}

\displaystyle\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathsf{Y}f\,V$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}

\displaystyle=(\mathsf{step}^{\kappa}{}\circ\mathsf{next}^{\kappa})(\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle f(\mathsf{Y}f)\,V$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa})

7.
Couplings and Lifting relations

Our next goal is to define a logical relation between the syntax and semantics and use it to reason about contextual equivalence of
\mathsf{FPC}_{\oplus}
terms.
This is done by defining a value relation (of type
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle{\sigma}$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}\to\mathsf{Val}_{\mathsf{\sigma}}\to\mathsf{Prop}
) and
an expression relation (of type
\mathsf{D}^{\kappa}{}{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle{\sigma}$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}}\to\mathsf{D}^{\!\forall\!}{}(\mathsf{Val}_{\mathsf{\sigma}})\to\mathsf{Prop}
) by mutual recursion.
We start by defining a relational lifting
(
Katsumata and Sato, 2013
)
operation mapping a relation

{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}:A\to B\to\mathsf{Prop}
on values and to a relation
{}\,{}\overline{{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}}^{\kappa}\,{}:\mathsf{D}^{\kappa}{A}\to\mathsf{D}^{\!\forall\!}{B}\to\mathsf{Prop}
on computations.
This will be used to define the expression relation as the relational lifting of the value relation.
As opposed to standard lifting constructions, that consider the same monad on both sides of the relation, there is an asymmetry in the type of
{}\,{}\overline{{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}}^{\kappa}\,{}
.
The reason is that the logical relation will be defined by guarded recursion in the first argument, while using arbitrarily deep unfoldings of the term on the right-hand side.
This section defines the relational lifting construction and proves its basic properties.

The first step is lifting a relation
{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}
over
A\times B
to a relation over
\mathcal{D}{A}\times\mathcal{D}{B}
.
Recall the notion of
{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}
-coupling
(
Lindvall, 2002
;
Barthe et al., 2009
)
, which achieves this precise end:

Definition 7.1.

Let
{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}:A\to B\to\mathsf{Prop}
, and let
\mu:\mathcal{D}A,\nu:\mathcal{D}B
be finite distributions. An
{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}
-coupling between
\mu
and
\nu
is a distribution on the total space of
{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}
,
i.e.
,
\rho:\mathcal{D}(\Sigma(a\!:\!A),(b\!:\!B).a{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}b)
, whose marginals are
\mu
and
\nu
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}{(\mathsf{pr}_{1})}(\rho)

\displaystyle=\mu

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}{(\mathsf{pr}_{2})}(\rho)=\nu,

where
\mathsf{pr}_{1}
and
\mathsf{pr}_{2}
are the projection maps
A\xleftarrow{\mathsf{pr}_{1}}A\times B\xrightarrow{\mathsf{pr}_{2}}B
.
We write
\mathsf{Cpl}_{{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}}(\mu,\nu)
for the type of
{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}
-couplings between
\mu
and
\nu
.

In other words, an
{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}
-coupling is a joint distribution over
A\times B
with the property that
{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}
always holds for any pair of
values sampled from it.
Our definition of
\mu{}\,{}\overline{{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}}^{\kappa}\,{}\nu
will generalise this idea for pairs of computations, whose final
values might become available at different times.
Recall that by Theorem
3.6
a distribution

\mu:\mathsf{D}^{\kappa}{A}\simeq\mathcal{D}(A+\triangleright^{\kappa}(\mathsf{D}^{\kappa}A))
must be either a distribution of values, one
of delayed computations or a convex combination of the two.
The relation
\mu{}\,{}\overline{{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}}^{\kappa}\,{}\nu
should then hold if (1) the values available
in
\mu
now can be matched by
\nu
after possibly some computation steps, and (2) the
delayed computation part of
\mu
can be matched later, in a guarded recursive step. The matching
of values is done via an
{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}
-coupling, and the computation steps are interpreted using
\leadsto^{\approx}
. We choose
\leadsto^{\approx}
over the more straightforward
\leadsto
to allow the matching values for
\mu
to be delivered in the limit of an infinite sequence of computation steps. By choosing
\leadsto^{\approx}
, we just require that any fraction of it can be delivered in finite time.
In the following definition, we leave the inclusions
\mathcal{D}(\mathsf{inl})
and
\mathcal{D}(\mathsf{inr})
implicit and simply write, e.g.,
\mu:\mathcal{D}{A}
for the first
case mentioned above.

Definition 7.2 (Lifting of Relations).

Given a relation
{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}:A\to B\to\mathsf{Prop}
we define its lift
{}\,{}\overline{{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}}^{\kappa}\,{}:\mathsf{D}^{\kappa}{A}\to\mathsf{D}^{\!\forall\!}{B}\to\mathsf{Prop}
as:

\mu{}\,{}\overline{{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}}^{\kappa}\,{}\nu
if one of the following three options is true:

(1)

\mu:\mathcal{D}{A}
, there is a
\nu^{\prime}:\mathcal{D}{B}
such that
\nu\leadsto^{\approx}\nu^{\prime}
, and there is a
\rho:\mathsf{Cpl}_{{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}}(\mu,\nu^{\prime})
.

(2)

\mu:\mathcal{D}(\triangleright^{\kappa}{}\mathsf{D}^{\kappa}{A})
and
\triangleright^{\kappa}{(\alpha:\kappa)}\ (((\zeta^{\kappa}{(}\mu))[\alpha]){}\,{}\overline{{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}}^{\kappa}\,{}\nu)
,

where
\zeta^{\kappa}{\,}:\mathcal{D}(\triangleright^{\kappa}{}\mathsf{D}^{\kappa}{A})\rightarrow\triangleright^{\kappa}{}\mathsf{D}^{\kappa}{A}
is defined as

\displaystyle\zeta^{\kappa}{(}\delta(x))

\displaystyle\triangleq x

\displaystyle\zeta^{\kappa}{(}{\mu}\oplus_{p}{\nu})

\displaystyle\triangleq\char 12\relax{\alpha}{\kappa}{\zeta^{\kappa}{(}\mu)\,[\alpha]}\oplus^{\kappa}_{p}{\zeta^{\kappa}{(}\nu)\,[\alpha]}

(3)

There exist
\mu_{1}:\mathcal{D}{A},\mu_{2}:\mathcal{D}(\triangleright^{\kappa}{}\mathsf{D}^{\kappa}{A})
, and
p\in(0,1)
such that
\mu={\mu_{1}}\oplus_{p}{\mu_{2}}
, there exist
\nu_{1},\nu_{2}:\mathsf{D}^{\!\forall\!}{B}
such that
\nu\leadsto^{\approx}{\nu_{1}}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{\nu_{2}}
, and
\mu_{1}{}\,{}\overline{{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}}^{\kappa}\,{}\nu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}{}\,{}\overline{{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}}^{\kappa}\,{}\nu_{2}
.

We then define
{}\,{}\overline{{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}}\,{}:\mathsf{D}^{\!\forall\!}{A}\to\mathsf{D}^{\!\forall\!}{B}\to\mathsf{Prop}
as:

\mu{}\,{}\overline{{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}}\,{}\nu\triangleq\forall\kappa.(\mu[\kappa]{}\,{}\overline{{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}}^{\kappa}\,{}\nu).

The following is a consequence of the encoding of coinductive types using guarded recursion.

Lemma 7.3.

If
A
is clock irrelevant, then

{}\,{}\overline{{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}}\,{}
is the coinductive solution to the equation that
\mu{}\,{}\overline{{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}}\,{}\nu
if and only if
one of the following:

(1)

\mu:\mathcal{D}{A}
, there is a
\nu^{\prime}:\mathcal{D}{B}
such that
\nu\leadsto^{\approx}\nu^{\prime}
, and there is a
\rho:\mathsf{Cpl}_{{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}}(\mu,\nu^{\prime})
.

(2)

\mu:\mathcal{D}(\mathsf{D}^{\!\forall\!}{A})
and
\mathsf{run}(\mu){}\,{}\overline{{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}}\,{}\nu
.

(3)

There exist
\mu_{1}:\mathcal{D}{A},\mu_{2}:\mathcal{D}(\mathsf{D}^{\!\forall\!}{A})
, and
p:(0,1)
such that

\mu={\mu_{1}}\oplus_{p}{\mu_{2}}
, there exist
\nu_{1},\nu_{2}:\mathsf{D}^{\!\forall\!}{B}
such that
\nu\leadsto^{\approx}{\nu_{1}}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{\nu_{2}}
, and
\mu_{1}{}\,{}\overline{{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}}\,{}\nu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}{}\,{}\overline{{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}}\,{}\nu_{2}
.

The following lemma allows us to use existence of a lifting to prove an inequality between probabilities of termination, which will be useful when reasoning about contextual refinement

Lemma 7.4.

Let
\mu,\nu:\mathsf{D}^{\!\forall\!}{1}
, and let
\mathsf{eq}_{1}:1\to 1\to\mathsf{Prop}
be the identity relation, relating the unique element to itself. Then,

\mu{}\,{}\overline{\mathsf{eq}_{1}}\,{}\nu\Rightarrow\mathsf{PT}_{(.)}(\mu)\leq_{\mathrm{lim}}\mathsf{PT}_{(.)}(\nu).

In the remainder of this section, we will prove some useful reasoning principles for working with
{}\,{}\overline{{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}}^{\kappa}\,{}
