at a more abstract level, without the need for unfolding the definition.
These are summarized in Figure
5
, where the double bar indicates that the rule
is bidirectional.

\frac{\nu\leadsto\nu^{\prime}\mu{}\,{}\overline{{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}}^{\kappa}\,{}\nu}{\mu{}\,{}\overline{{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}}^{\kappa}\,{}\nu^{\prime}}\frac{\nu\leadsto\nu^{\prime}\mu{}\,{}\overline{{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}}^{\kappa}\,{}\nu^{\prime}}{\mu{}\,{}\overline{{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}}^{\kappa}\,{}\nu}\frac{\nu\leadsto^{\approx}\nu^{\prime}\mu{}\,{}\overline{{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}}^{\kappa}\,{}\nu^{\prime}}{\mu{}\,{}\overline{{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}}^{\kappa}\,{}\nu}{\frac{{(\mathsf{step}^{\kappa}\mu_{1})}\oplus^{\kappa}_{p}{(\mathsf{step}^{\kappa}\mu_{2})}{}\,{}\overline{{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}}^{\kappa}\,{}\nu}{\mathsf{step}^{\kappa}(\char 12\relax{\alpha}{\kappa}{(\mu_{1}\,[\alpha])}\oplus^{\kappa}_{p}{(\mu_{2}\,[\alpha])}){}\,{}\overline{{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}}^{\kappa}\,{}\nu}}{\frac{\mu{}\,{}\overline{{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}}^{\kappa}\,{}\mathsf{step}^{\!\forall\!}({\nu_{1}}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{\nu_{2}})}{\mu{}\,{}\overline{{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}}^{\kappa}\,{}{(\mathsf{step}^{\!\forall\!}(\nu_{1}))}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{(\mathsf{step}^{\!\forall\!}(\nu_{2}))}}}\frac{\mu_{1}{}\,{}\overline{{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}}^{\kappa}\,{}\nu_{1}\mu_{2}{}\,{}\overline{{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}}^{\kappa}\,{}\nu_{2}}{({\mu_{1}}\oplus^{\kappa}_{p}{\mu_{2}}){}\,{}\overline{{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}}^{\kappa}\,{}({\nu_{1}}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{\nu_{2}})}\frac{\mu{}\,{}\overline{{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}}^{\kappa}\,{}\overline{\nu}\forall a,b.a{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}b\rightarrow f(a){}\,{}\overline{\mathcal{S}}^{\kappa}\,{}g(b)}{\overline{f}(\mu){}\,{}\overline{\mathcal{S}}^{\kappa}\,{}\overline{g}(\nu)}

Figure 5.
Reasoning principles for
{}\,{}\overline{{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}}^{\kappa}\,{}

First we show that
{}\,{}\overline{{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}}^{\kappa}\,{}
is invariant with respect to
\leadsto
and
\leadsto^{\approx}
-reductions on its second
argument.

Lemma 7.5.

If
\nu\leadsto\nu^{\prime}
then
\mu{}\,{}\overline{{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}}^{\kappa}\,{}\nu
iff
\mu{}\,{}\overline{{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}}^{\kappa}\,{}\nu^{\prime}
.
In particular
\mu{}\,{}\overline{{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}}^{\kappa}\,{}(\mathsf{step}^{\!\forall\!}{}\nu)
iff
\mu{}\,{}\overline{{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}}^{\kappa}\,{}\nu
.

Proof.

Left to right follows from Lemma
4.10
, and right to left is by Lemma
4.19
.
∎

Lemma 7.6.

If
\nu\leadsto^{\approx}\nu^{\prime}
and
\mu{}\,{}\overline{{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}}^{\kappa}\,{}\nu^{\prime}
, then also
\mu{}\,{}\overline{{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}}^{\kappa}\,{}\nu
.

Proof.

This follows from transitivity of
\leadsto^{\approx}
.
∎

The lemma below allows us to commute computation steps with probabilistic choices
on either side of a lifted relation:

Lemma 7.7.

Let
{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}:A\to B\to\mathsf{Prop}
.

(1)

If
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}:\triangleright^{\kappa}\mathsf{D}^{\kappa}{A}
,
\nu:\mathsf{D}^{\!\forall\!}{B}
then

{(\mathsf{step}^{\kappa}\mu_{1})}\oplus^{\kappa}_{p}{(\mathsf{step}^{\kappa}\mu_{2})}{}\,{}\overline{{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}}^{\kappa}\,{}\nu\text{ iff }\mathsf{step}^{\kappa}(\char 12\relax{\alpha}{\kappa}{(\mu_{1}\,[\alpha])}\oplus^{\kappa}_{p}{(\mu_{2}\,[\alpha])}){}\,{}\overline{{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}}^{\kappa}\,{}\nu.

(2)

If
\mu:\mathsf{D}^{\kappa}{A}
and
\nu_{1},\nu_{2}:\mathsf{D}^{\!\forall\!}{B}
then
\mu{}\,{}\overline{{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}}^{\kappa}\,{}\mathsf{step}^{\!\forall\!}({\nu_{1}}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{\nu_{2}})
iff

\mu{}\,{}\overline{{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}}^{\kappa}\,{}{(\mathsf{step}^{\!\forall\!}(\nu_{1}))}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{(\mathsf{step}^{\!\forall\!}(\nu_{2}))}
.

Proof.

The first statement follows from applying Definition
7.2
(
2
). The second statement follows from Lemma
7.5
.
∎

Liftings are a useful technique to reason about computations because of the way they
interact with choice, and with the monad structures of
\mathsf{D}^{\kappa}
and
\mathsf{D}^{\!\forall\!}
.
First, liftings are closed under choice operators, which allows us to construct them
by parts:

Lemma 7.8.

Let
{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}:A\to B\to\mathsf{Prop}
, and suppose that
\mu_{1}{}\,{}\overline{{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}}^{\kappa}\,{}\nu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}{}\,{}\overline{{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}}^{\kappa}\,{}\nu_{2}
. Then also

({\mu_{1}}\oplus^{\kappa}_{p}{\mu_{2}}){}\,{}\overline{{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}}^{\kappa}\,{}({\nu_{1}}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{\nu_{2}})
.

Proof (sketch).

There are 9 cases to consider, one for each of the possible combinations of cases for
\mu_{1}{}\,{}\overline{{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}}^{\kappa}\,{}\nu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}{}\,{}\overline{{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}}^{\kappa}\,{}\nu_{2}
. The proof for each of these cases follows quite directly from the assumptions that we get from
\mu_{1}{}\,{}\overline{{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}}^{\kappa}\,{}\nu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}{}\,{}\overline{{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}}^{\kappa}\,{}\nu_{2}
, after some rewriting of terms using the axioms of convex algebras. For example, if
\mu_{1}:\mathcal{D}{A}
and for
\mu_{2}
there exist
\mu_{21}:\mathcal{D}{A},\mu_{22}:\mathcal{D}(\triangleright^{\kappa}{}\mathsf{D}^{\kappa}{A})
such that
\mu_{2}={\mu_{21}}\oplus_{p^{\prime}}{\mu_{22}}
, then:

\displaystyle{\mu_{1}}\oplus^{\kappa}_{p}{\mu_{2}}

\displaystyle={\mu_{1}}\oplus^{\kappa}_{p}{({\mu_{21}}\oplus^{\kappa}_{p^{\prime}}{\mu_{22}})}={({\mu_{1}}\oplus^{\kappa}_{\frac{p}{p+(1-p)p^{\prime}}}{\mu_{21}})}\oplus^{\kappa}_{p+(1-p)p^{\prime}}{\mu_{22}},

where
{\mu_{1}}\oplus^{\kappa}_{\frac{p}{p+(1-p)p^{\prime}}}{\mu_{21}}:\mathcal{D}{A}
and
\mu_{22}:\mathcal{D}(\triangleright^{\kappa}{}\mathsf{D}^{\kappa}{A})
.
We can then combine information coming from the assumptions
\mu_{1}{}\,{}\overline{{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}}^{\kappa}\,{}\nu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}{}\,{}\overline{{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}}^{\kappa}\,{}\nu_{2}
using the same probabilities as above to reach the desired conclusion.
∎

Second, we can prove a bind lemma, that allows us to sequence computations
related by liftings. This lemma will be crucial e.g. in the proof of the
fundamental lemma in the following section.

Lemma 7.9 (Bind Lemma).

Let
{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}:A\to B\to\mathsf{Prop}
and
\mathcal{S}:A^{\prime}\to B^{\prime}\to\mathsf{Prop}
.

(1)

If
f:A\to\mathsf{D}^{\kappa}{A^{\prime}}
and
g:B\to\mathsf{D}^{\!\forall\!}{B^{\prime}}
satisfy
f(a){}\,{}\overline{\mathcal{S}}^{\kappa}\,{}g(b)
whenever
a{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}b
, then for all

\mu:\mathcal{D}{A}
and
\nu:\mathcal{D}{B}
, if there is a
\rho:\mathsf{Cpl}_{{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}}(\mu,\nu)
, then

\overline{f}(\mu){}\,{}\overline{\mathcal{S}}^{\kappa}\,{}\overline{g}(\nu)
.

(2)

If
f:A\to\mathsf{D}^{\kappa}{A^{\prime}}
and
g:B\to\mathsf{D}^{\!\forall\!}{B^{\prime}}
satisfy
f(a){}\,{}\overline{\mathcal{S}}^{\kappa}\,{}g(b)
whenever
a{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}b
, then for all

\mu:\mathsf{D}^{\kappa}{A}
and
\nu:\mathsf{D}^{\!\forall\!}{B}
satisfying
\mu{}\,{}\overline{{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}}^{\kappa}\,{}\overline{\nu}
, also

\overline{f}(\mu){}\,{}\overline{\mathcal{S}}^{\kappa}\,{}\overline{g}(\nu)
.

Proof (sketch).

The first statement is by induction on the coupling
\rho:\mathsf{Cpl}_{{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}}(\mu,\nu)
.

For the second statement, suppose that
\mu{}\,{}\overline{{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}}^{\kappa}\,{}\nu
.
By the definition of
{}\,{}\overline{{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}}^{\kappa}\,{}\!\!
, there are three cases to consider.
We only show the first case here. The second case is by guarded recursion, and the third case follows from the first and second cases.

For the first case, we assume that
\mu:\mathcal{D}{A}
, that there exist
\nu_{1}:\mathcal{D}{B}
such that
\nu\leadsto^{\approx}\nu_{1}
, and that there exists a
\rho:\mathsf{Cpl}_{{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}}(\mu,\nu_{1})
. What we want is to show that
\overline{f}(\mu){}\,{}\overline{\mathcal{S}}^{\kappa}\,{}\overline{g}(\nu)
.

Notice that since the
\leadsto^{\approx}
relation is preserved by homomorphisms (Lemma
4.18
),
\nu\leadsto^{\approx}\nu_{1}
implies
\overline{g}(\nu)\leadsto^{\approx}\overline{g}(\nu_{1})
. By Lemma
7.6
it is therefore enough to show that
\overline{f}(\mu){}\,{}\overline{\mathcal{S}}^{\kappa}\,{}\overline{g}(\nu_{1})
, which follows directly from the first statement of this lemma.

∎

8.
Relating syntax and semantics

In this section we prove that our denotational semantics is adequate with
respect to the operational semantics. The overall approach is standard in that
we define type-indexed logical relations between semantic denotations and terms
of the operational semantics, and we define both value relations and expression (aka
term or computation) relations:

\displaystyle{}\,\preceq^{\kappa,{\sf Val}}_{\sigma}\,{}

\displaystyle:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle{\sigma}$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}\to\mathsf{Val}_{\mathsf{\sigma}}\to\mathsf{Prop}

\displaystyle{}\,\preceq^{\kappa,{\sf Tm}}_{\sigma}\,{}

\displaystyle:\mathsf{D}^{\kappa}{}{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle{\sigma}$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}}\to\mathsf{D}^{\!\forall\!}{}(\mathsf{Val}_{\mathsf{\sigma}})\to\mathsf{Prop}

There are two things to note, however. The first is that traditionally it is
tricky to define such logical relations for a programming language with
recursive types; see, e.g.,
(
Pitts, 1996
)
. Here we follow

(
Møgelberg and Paviotti, 2019
)
and simply use guarded recursion (and
induction on types), see Figure
6
. Notice in particular
the case for recursive types, where guarded recursion is used. The second point
to note is that the definition of the term relation is novel: it is defined as the

lifting
(in the sense of the previous section) of the value relation.
This allows us to use the reasoning principles associated with lifting (e.g.,
Lemmas
7.5
,
7.8

and
7.9
) in proofs and examples.

Value relation

\frac{}{n{}\,\preceq^{\kappa,{\sf Val}}_{\mathsf{Nat}}\,{}\underline{n}}\frac{}{\star{}\,\preceq^{\kappa,{\sf Val}}_{1}\,{}\langle\rangle}\frac{v{}\,\preceq^{\kappa,{\sf Val}}_{\sigma}\,{}Vw{}\,\preceq^{\kappa,{\sf Val}}_{\tau}\,{}W}{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle{v},{w}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{}\,\preceq^{\kappa,{\sf Val}}_{{\sigma}\times{\tau}}\,{}\langle{V},{W}\rangle}\frac{v{}\,\preceq^{\kappa,{\sf Val}}_{\sigma}\,{}V}{{{\smash{\mathbf{inl}}}}\,{v}{}\,\preceq^{\kappa,{\sf Val}}_{{\sigma}+{\tau}}\,{}\mathsf{inl}{\,V}}\frac{v{}\,\preceq^{\kappa,{\sf Val}}_{\tau}\,{}V}{{{\smash{\mathbf{inr}}}}\,{v}{}\,\preceq^{\kappa,{\sf Val}}_{{\sigma}+{\tau}}\,{}\mathsf{inr}{\,V}}\frac{\forall w,V.w{}\,\preceq^{\kappa,{\sf Val}}_{\sigma}\,{}V\to v(w){}\,\preceq^{\kappa,{\sf Tm}}_{\tau}\,{}\mathsf{eval}{(M[V/x])}}{v{}\,\preceq^{\kappa,{\sf Val}}_{{\sigma}\to{\tau}}\,{}\mathsf{lam}\ {x}.{M}}\frac{\triangleright\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\alpha:\kappa$}\mathclose{}{)}}.{(v[\alpha]{}\,\preceq^{\kappa,{\sf Val}}_{\tau[\mu{X}.{\tau}/X]}\,{}V)}}{v{}\,\preceq^{\kappa,{\sf Val}}_{\mu{X}.{\tau}}\,{}\mathsf{fold}{\,V}}

Expression relation

\mu{}\,\preceq^{\kappa,{\sf Tm}}_{\sigma}\,{}d\triangleq\mu{}\,{}\overline{{}\,\preceq^{\kappa,{\sf Val}}_{\sigma}\,{}}^{\kappa}\,{}d

Figure 6.
Logical Relation.

We extend the expression relation to open terms in the standard way by using related environments and closing substitutions:

Definition 8.1.

For
M,N:\mathsf{Tm}^{\Gamma}_{\mathsf{\sigma}}
we define

M\preceq^{\kappa,\Gamma}_{\sigma}N\triangleq\left(\forall\rho,\delta.(\rho{}\,\preceq^{\kappa,{\sf Val}}_{\Gamma}\,{}\delta)\to\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle M$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}_{\rho}{}\,\preceq^{\kappa,{\sf Tm}}_{\sigma}\,{}\mathsf{eval}{(N[\delta])}\right)

Here
\delta
is a closing substitution for
\Gamma
and
\rho:\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle{\Gamma}$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}

an environment, and
\rho{}\,\preceq^{\kappa,{\sf Val}}_{\Gamma}\,{}\delta
denotes that for every
variable
x
of
\Gamma
the corresponding semantic and syntactic value in

\rho
and
\delta
are related.

One can now show that the logical relation is a congruence and that it is reflexive (the fundamental lemma holds).
Both these are proved by induction on
C
and
M
respectively.

Lemma 8.2 (Congruence Lemma).

For any terms
M,N:\mathsf{Tm}^{\Gamma}_{\mathsf{\sigma}}
and every context
C:({\Gamma}\vdash{\sigma})\Rightarrow({\Delta}\vdash{\tau})
we get that

M\preceq^{\kappa,\Gamma}_{\sigma}N\to C[M]\preceq^{\kappa,\Delta}_{\tau}C[N]

Lemma 8.3 (Fundamental Lemma).

For all
M:\mathsf{Tm}^{\Gamma}_{\mathsf{\sigma}}
we have that

M\preceq^{\kappa,\Gamma}_{\sigma}M.

To prove soundness of the logical relation for contextual equivalence, it only remains to relate it to
termination probability at the unit type. To begin, we use our logical relation to prove Theorem
6.1

presented in Section
6
:

Proof of Theorem
6.1
.

Recall that our goal is to show that
\mathsf{PT}_{(.)}(\mathsf{eval}\,M)=_{\mathrm{lim}}\mathsf{PT}_{(.)}(\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle M$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}})
for any
M:\mathsf{Tm}_{\mathsf{1}}
.
One direction follows directly from Lemma
8.3
: Since
\forall\kappa.\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle M$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}{}\,\preceq^{\kappa,{\sf Tm}}_{1}\,{}\mathsf{eval}{\,M}
, by
Lemma
7.4
also
\mathsf{PT}_{(.)}(\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle M$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}})\leq_{\mathrm{lim}}\mathsf{PT}_{(.)}(\mathsf{eval}\,M)
. For the other direction, we
define an alternative denotational semantics
\llparenthesis{-}\rrparenthesis
, which agrees with
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle-$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}
on types and most terms, but which uses the same steps as
the operational semantics, so for example,

\displaystyle\llparenthesis{MN}\rrparenthesis^{\kappa}_{\rho}\triangleq\llparenthesis{M}\rrparenthesis^{\kappa}_{\rho}>\!>\!=^{\kappa}\lambda f.\llparenthesis{N}\rrparenthesis^{\kappa}_{\rho}>\!>\!=^{\kappa}\lambda v.\mathsf{step}^{\kappa}(\char 12\relax\alpha\kappa fv)

and similarly for the interpretation of elimination for sum types. Since the steps agree, it is easy to show the following soundness result for

\llparenthesis{-}\rrparenthesis
: For any well typed closed expression
\cdot\vdash M:\sigma
we have that

\mathsf{D}^{\kappa}\!\!\left(\llparenthesis{-}\rrparenthesis^{\mathsf{Val},\kappa}\right)(\mathsf{eval}^{\kappa}M)\equiv\llparenthesis{M}\rrparenthesis^{\kappa}

and as a consequence
\mathsf{PT}_{(.)}(\mathsf{eval}\,M)=\mathsf{PT}_{(.)}(\llparenthesis{M}\rrparenthesis{})
. Finally, we
construct a logical relation on the denotation of types to show that
\mathsf{PT}_{(.)}(\llparenthesis{M}\rrparenthesis{})\leq_{\mathrm{lim}}\mathsf{PT}_{(.)}(\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle M$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}{})
.
∎

As a consequence, the logical relation implies relatedness of the termination probabilities.

Corollary 8.4.

For any
M,N:\mathsf{Tm}_{\mathsf{1}}
we have that

(\forall\kappa.\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle M$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}{}\,\preceq^{\kappa,{\sf Tm}}_{1}\,{}\mathsf{eval}{(N)})\to\mathsf{PT}_{(.)}(\mathsf{eval}{(M)})\leq_{\mathrm{lim}}\mathsf{PT}_{(.)}(\mathsf{eval}(N))

Proof.

By Lemma
7.4
,
\mathsf{PT}_{(.)}(\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle M$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}})\leq_{\mathrm{lim}}\mathsf{PT}_{(.)}(\mathsf{eval}(N))
, and so the
result follows from Theorem
6.1
.
∎

Combining this with the Congruence Lemma,
we get that the logical
relation is sound with respect to contextual refinement:

Theorem 8.5.

For any terms
\Gamma\vdash M:\sigma
and
\Gamma\vdash N:\sigma
we have

(\forall\kappa.M\preceq^{\kappa,\Gamma}_{\sigma}N)\to M\preceq_{\mathsf{Ctx}}N

As a corollary, we obtain computational adequacy (using the notation
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle M$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}\triangleq\Lambda\kappa.\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle M$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}
):

Theorem 8.6 (Adequacy).

Let
M,N
be terms of the same type. If
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle M$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}=\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle N$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}
then
M\equiv_{\mathsf{Ctx}}N
.

Proof.

By Lemma
8.3
we have
\forall\kappa.N\preceq^{\kappa,\Gamma}_{\sigma}N
, since

\kappa
is free in the statement of the lemma. So, since

\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle M$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}=\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle N$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}
also
\forall\kappa.M\preceq^{\kappa,\Gamma}_{\sigma}N
. By Theorem
8.5

then also
M\preceq_{\mathsf{Ctx}}N
. The other direction is symmetric.
∎

9.
Examples

We now give a series of examples to illustrate how Theorem
8.5
can be used for reasoning about
\mathsf{FPC}_{\oplus}
. All the examples presented are variants
of similar examples in the literature
(
Bizjak and Birkedal, 2015
;
Aguirre et al., 2018
)
. Our point is to show how these examples can be done in constructive type theory,
and to illustrate the simplicity of these in our abstract viewpoint. We choose to work directly with the logical relation
\preceq^{\kappa,\Gamma}_{\sigma}
between syntax and semantics.
An alternative could be to use a relation on denotational semantics, as the one used in the proof of Theorem
6.1
. One could have also
defined a relation directly from syntax to syntax, but the additional steps in the operational semantics would have cluttered the proofs. We return to this point in
Remark
3
.

We start by showing that the reduction relation
\leadsto
is contained in contextual equivalence.

Theorem 9.1.

Let
M
and
N
be closed terms of the same type
\sigma
.

(1)

If
\mathsf{eval}\,M\leadsto\mathsf{eval}\,N
, then
M\equiv_{\mathsf{Ctx}}N
.

(2)

If
\mathsf{eval}\,M\leadsto^{\approx}\mathsf{eval}\,N
, then
N\preceq_{\mathsf{Ctx}}M
.

Proof.

For (
1
), by Lemma
7.5
,
\mu{}\,\preceq^{\kappa,{\sf Val}}_{\sigma}\,{}\mathsf{eval}\,M
if and only if
\mu{}\,\preceq^{\kappa,{\sf Val}}_{\sigma}\,{}\mathsf{eval}\,N
. By
Lemma
8.3

\forall\kappa.\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle M$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}{}\,\preceq^{\kappa,{\sf Val}}_{\sigma}\,{}\mathsf{eval}\,M
, so
\forall\kappa.\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle M$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}{}\,\preceq^{\kappa,{\sf Val}}_{\sigma}\,{}\mathsf{eval}\,N
, and so
by Theorem
8.5
also
M\preceq_{\mathsf{Ctx}}N
. The other way is similar. The second statement is proved similarly using Lemma
7.6
.
∎

For example, since
\mathsf{eval}{(\mathsf{Y}f\,V)}\leadsto\mathsf{eval}{(f(\mathsf{Y}f)V)}
, also

\mathsf{Y}f\,V\equiv_{\mathsf{Ctx}}f(\mathsf{Y}f)\,V
. Similarly, since

\displaystyle\mathsf{eval}((\mathsf{lam}\ {x}.{M})\,V)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{step}^{\!\forall\!}(\mathsf{eval}(M[V/x]))\leadsto\mathsf{eval}(M[V/x])

\displaystyle\mathsf{eval}(\mathsf{unfold}{\,(\mathsf{fold}{\,V})})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{step}^{\!\forall\!}(V)\leadsto V

for closed values
V
and
\mathsf{lam}\ {x}.{M}
,
we get the usual call-by-value
\beta
rules up to contextual equivalence.

9.1.
A hesitant identity function

For any type
\sigma
and rational number
p:(0,1)
, define the hesitant identity function
\mathsf{hid_{p}}:\sigma\to\sigma

as

\displaystyle{\mathsf{hid_{p}}}

\displaystyle\triangleq\mathsf{lam}\ {z}.{\mathsf{Y}\,\mathsf{hid^{\prime}_{p}}\,z}

where

\displaystyle\mathsf{hid^{\prime}_{p}}

\displaystyle\triangleq\mathsf{lam}\ {f}.{\mathsf{lam}\ {x}.{\mathsf{choice}^{p}(x,fx)}}

Given an
x
,
\mathsf{hid_{p}}
makes a probabilistic choice between immediately returning
x
and calling itself recursively.
We will show that
{\mathsf{hid_{p}}}\equiv_{\mathsf{Ctx}}{\sf id}
, starting with

{\sf id}\preceq_{\mathsf{Ctx}}{\mathsf{hid_{p}}}
.

Since
\mathsf{hid_{p}}
is a value, it suffices to show that

(9)

\delta^{\kappa}{(v)}{}\,\preceq^{\kappa,{\sf Tm}}_{\sigma}\,{}\mathsf{eval}{(\mathsf{hid_{p}}\,V)}

for all
v,V
satisfying
v{}\,\preceq^{\kappa,{\sf Val}}_{\sigma}\,{}V
. Now,
\mathsf{eval}{(\mathsf{hid_{p}}\,V)}\leadsto\mathsf{eval}{(\mathsf{Y}\,\mathsf{hid^{\prime}_{p}}\,V)}
,
and by definition of
\mathsf{Y}
,
\mathsf{Y}\,\mathsf{hid^{\prime}_{p}}
reduces in one step to a value, which we shall simply write
W_{\mathsf{hid,p}}{}
for. To prove
(
9
) it therefore suffices to prove

(10)

\delta^{\kappa}{(v)}{}\,\preceq^{\kappa,{\sf Tm}}_{\sigma}\,{}\mathsf{eval}{(W_{\mathsf{hid,p}}{}\,V)}

Since the reduction
\mathsf{eval}{(\mathsf{Y}\,\mathsf{hid^{\prime}_{p}}\,V)}\leadsto\mathsf{eval}{(\mathsf{hid^{\prime}_{p}}\,(\mathsf{Y}\,\mathsf{hid^{\prime}_{p}})\,V)}
factors through

W_{\mathsf{hid,p}}{}\,V

it follows that

\displaystyle\mathsf{eval}{(W_{\mathsf{hid,p}}{}\,V)}

\displaystyle\leadsto\mathsf{eval}{(\mathsf{hid^{\prime}_{p}}\,(\mathsf{Y}\,\mathsf{hid^{\prime}_{p}})\,V)}

\displaystyle\leadsto\mathsf{eval}{(\mathsf{hid^{\prime}_{p}}\,W_{\mathsf{hid,p}}{}\,V)}

\displaystyle\leadsto\mathsf{eval}{(\mathsf{choice}^{p}(V,W_{\mathsf{hid,p}}{}\,V))}

\displaystyle={\delta^{\forall\!}V}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{\mathsf{eval}{(W_{\mathsf{hid,p}}{}\,V)}}

By Example
4.16
then
\mathsf{eval}{(W_{\mathsf{hid,p}}\,V)}\leadsto^{\approx}\delta^{\forall\!}V
, so that
(
10
) follows from Lemma
7.6
.

To prove
{\mathsf{hid_{p}}}\preceq_{\mathsf{Ctx}}{\sf id}
, since
\mathsf{hid_{p}}
is a value, it suffices to show that

(11)

\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathsf{hid_{p}}$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\mathsf{Val},\kappa}(v){}\,\preceq^{\kappa,{\sf Tm}}_{\sigma}\,{}\delta^{\forall\!}{(V)}

for all
v,V
satisfying
v{}\,\preceq^{\kappa,{\sf Val}}_{\sigma}\,{}V
.
Using (
8
) we compute

\displaystyle\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathsf{hid_{p}}$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\mathsf{Val},\kappa}(v)

\displaystyle=\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathsf{Y}(\mathsf{hid^{\prime}_{p}})\,z$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}_{z\mapsto v}

\displaystyle=\Delta^{\kappa}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathsf{hid^{\prime}_{p}}(\mathsf{Y}(\mathsf{hid^{\prime}_{p}}))\,z$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}_{z\mapsto v}

\displaystyle=\Delta^{\kappa}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathsf{choice}^{p}(x,fx)$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}_{x\mapsto v,f\mapsto\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle W_{\mathsf{hid,p}}$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\mathsf{Val},\kappa}}

\displaystyle=\Delta^{\kappa}({\delta^{\kappa}{v}}\oplus^{\kappa}_{p}{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathsf{hid_{p}}$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\mathsf{Val},\kappa}(v)})

so that (
11
) is equivalent to

(12)

\triangleright^{\kappa}({\delta^{\kappa}{v}}\oplus^{\kappa}_{p}{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathsf{hid_{p}}$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\mathsf{Val},\kappa}(v)}{}\,\preceq^{\kappa,{\sf Tm}}_{\sigma}\,{}\delta^{\forall\!}{(V)})

and can therefore be easily proved by guarded recursion using Lemma
7.7
.

Remark 3.

Note the benefit of having the denotational semantics on the left hand side of the relation in the example above: Unfolding (
11
) only leads to a single (outer)
\triangleright^{\kappa}
in (
12
).
Had we used operational semantics on the left hand side, the unfolding would have been cluttered by additional steps
intertwined with probabilistic choices.
On the right hand side, the additional steps disappear by the use of
\leadsto
.

9.2.
A fair coin from an unfair coin

Define
\mathsf{bool}\triangleq{1}+{1}
referring to the elements as
\mathsf{tt}
and
\mathsf{ff}
. A
coin
is a program
of the form
\mathsf{choice}^{p}(\mathsf{tt},\mathsf{ff}):\mathsf{bool}
for
p:(0,1)
. A coin is called
fair
if
p=\frac{1}{2}
.

Given a coin, one can encode a fair coin as follows: Toss the coin twice. If the results are different, return the first result, otherwise try again.
This idea can be encoded as the
\mathsf{FPC}_{\oplus}
term
\mathsf{efair_{p}}
:

\displaystyle\mathsf{efair_{p}}\triangleq\mathsf{Y}(\mathsf{efair^{\prime}_{p}}):{1}\to{\mathsf{bool}}

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathsf{efair^{\prime}_{p}}\triangleq\mathsf{lam}\ {g}.{}\mathsf{lam}\ {z}.{}&\mathsf{let}\ x=\mathsf{choice}^{p}(\mathsf{tt},\mathsf{ff})\\
&\mathsf{let}\ y=\mathsf{choice}^{p}(\mathsf{tt},\mathsf{ff})\\
&\mathsf{if}\ {{\sf eqbool}(x,y)}\ \mathsf{then}\ {g(z)}\ \mathsf{else}\ {x}\end{aligned}

We now prove that this indeed gives a fair coin.

Theorem 9.2.

\mathsf{efair_{p}}\langle\rangle
is contextually equivalent to
\mathsf{choice}^{\frac{1}{2}}(\mathsf{tt},\mathsf{ff})

Proof.

Let
{W_{\mathsf{efair,p}}}
be the value that
\mathsf{Y}(\mathsf{efair^{\prime}_{p}})
unfolds to.
Unfolding definitions shows that

\displaystyle\mathsf{eval}{(W_{\mathsf{efair,p}}\langle\rangle)}

\displaystyle\leadsto{({\mathsf{eval}(W_{\mathsf{efair,p}}\langle\rangle)}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{\delta^{\forall\!}\mathsf{tt}})\!}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{\!({\delta^{\forall\!}\mathsf{ff}}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{\mathsf{eval}(W_{\mathsf{efair,p}}\langle\rangle)})}

\displaystyle={({\delta^{\forall\!}\mathsf{tt}}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{\frac{1}{2}}{\delta^{\forall\!}\mathsf{ff}})}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{2p(1-p)}{\mathsf{eval}{(W_{\mathsf{efair,p}}\langle\rangle)}}

From this, it follows that
\mathsf{eval}{(W_{\mathsf{efair,p}}\langle\rangle)}\leadsto^{\approx}{\delta^{\forall\!}(\mathsf{tt})}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{\frac{1}{2}}{\delta^{\forall\!}(\mathsf{ff})}
as in Example
4.16
, and since

\mathsf{eval}{(\mathsf{efair_{p}}\langle\rangle)}\leadsto\mathsf{eval}{(W_{\mathsf{efair,p}}\langle\rangle)}
, also
\mathsf{choice}^{\frac{1}{2}}(\mathsf{tt},\mathsf{ff})\preceq_{\mathsf{Ctx}}\mathsf{efair_{p}}\langle\rangle
follows from Theorem
9.1
.

On the other hand, to show
\mathsf{efair_{p}}\langle\rangle\preceq_{\mathsf{Ctx}}\mathsf{choice}^{\frac{1}{2}}(\mathsf{tt},\mathsf{ff})
, since
\mathsf{efair_{p}}\leadsto W_{\mathsf{efair,p}}
, it suffices to show that

\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle W_{\mathsf{efair,p}}\langle\rangle$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}{}\,\preceq^{\kappa,{\sf Tm}}_{\mathsf{bool}}\,{}\mathsf{eval}{(\mathsf{choice}^{\frac{1}{2}}(\mathsf{tt},\mathsf{ff}))}
. Computing

\displaystyle\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle W_{\mathsf{efair,p}}\langle\rangle$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}

\displaystyle=\Delta^{\kappa}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathsf{efair^{\prime}_{p}}\,(\mathsf{Y}\,\mathsf{efair^{\prime}_{p}})\langle\rangle$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}

\displaystyle=\Delta^{\kappa}\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathsf{efair^{\prime}_{p}}\,W_{\mathsf{efair,p}}\,\langle\rangle$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}

\displaystyle=\Delta^{\kappa}\left({\left({\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle W_{\mathsf{efair,p}}\,\langle\rangle$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}}\oplus^{\kappa}_{p}{\delta^{\kappa}(\mathsf{tt})}\right)}\oplus^{\kappa}_{p}{\left({\delta^{\kappa}(\mathsf{ff})}\oplus^{\kappa}_{p}{(\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle W_{\mathsf{efair,p}}\,\langle\rangle$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa})}\right)}\right)

\displaystyle=\Delta^{\kappa}\left({\left({\delta^{\kappa}(\mathsf{tt})}\oplus^{\kappa}_{\frac{1}{2}}{\delta^{\kappa}(\mathsf{ff})}\right)}\oplus^{\kappa}_{2p(1-p)}{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle W_{\mathsf{efair,p}}\,\langle\rangle$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}}\right)

So that
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle W_{\mathsf{efair,p}}\langle\rangle$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}{}\,\preceq^{\kappa,{\sf Tm}}_{\mathsf{bool}}\,{}\mathsf{eval}{(\mathsf{choice}^{\frac{1}{2}}(\mathsf{tt},\mathsf{ff}))}

is equivalent to

\triangleright^{\kappa}\left({({\delta^{\kappa}(\mathsf{tt})}\oplus^{\kappa}_{\frac{1}{2}}{\delta^{\kappa}(\mathsf{ff})})}\oplus^{\kappa}_{2p(1-p)}{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle W_{\mathsf{efair,p}}\,\langle\rangle$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}}{}\,\preceq^{\kappa,{\sf Tm}}_{\mathsf{bool}}\,{}\mathsf{eval}{(\mathsf{choice}^{\frac{1}{2}}(\mathsf{tt},\mathsf{ff}))}\right)

and can therefore be proved by guarded recursion.
∎

9.3.
Random Walk

The next example, inspired by
(
Aguirre et al., 2018
)
, is an equivalence of two random walks.
We represent a random walk by its trace, which is a potentially infinite list of integers.
In a call-by-value language such terms are best implemented as
lazy lists
. We define
an
\mathsf{FPC}_{\oplus}
type of these as

\mathsf{LazyL}\triangleq\mu{X}.{1+\mathsf{Nat}\times({1}\to{X})}

as well as a familiar interface for programming with these in Figure
7
.

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\mathsf{nil}:\mathsf{LazyL}\\
&\mathsf{nil}\triangleq\mathsf{fold}{\,(\mathsf{inl}{\,(}\langle\rangle))}\\
\\
&\mathsf{hd}:\mathsf{LazyL}\to\mathsf{Nat}+1\\
&\mathsf{hd}\triangleq\mathsf{lam}\ {l}.{\mathsf{case}(\mathsf{unfold}{\,l},x.\mathsf{inr}{\,\langle\rangle},(n,f).\mathsf{inl}{\,\underline{n}})}\end{aligned}\qquad\qquad\begin{aligned} &\mathsf{tl}:\mathsf{LazyL}\to\mathsf{LazyL}\\
&\mathsf{tl}\triangleq\mathsf{lam}\ {l}.{\mathsf{case}(\mathsf{unfold}{\,l},x.\mathsf{nil},(n,f).f(\langle\rangle))}\\
\\
&{-}::{-}:\mathsf{Nat}\to({1}\to{\mathsf{LazyL}})\to\mathsf{LazyL}\\
&{-}::{-}\triangleq\mathsf{lam}\ {n}.{\mathsf{lam}\ {f}.{\mathsf{fold}{\,(\mathsf{inr}{\,(}n,f))}}}\end{aligned}

Figure 7.
Lazy List Interface

Using these we can define a lazy list processing function
\mathsf{2nd}:\mathsf{LazyL}\to\mathsf{LazyL}
, returning a lazy list that contains every second element of the argument.
Define
\mathsf{2nd}\triangleq\mathsf{Y}(G_{\mathsf{2nd}})
where

\displaystyle G_{\mathsf{2nd}}

\displaystyle:({\mathsf{LazyL}}\to{\mathsf{LazyL}})\to\mathsf{LazyL}\to\mathsf{LazyL}

\displaystyle G_{\mathsf{2nd}}

\displaystyle\triangleq\mathsf{lam}\ {g}.{\mathsf{lam}\ {l}.{\mathsf{case}(\mathsf{unfold}{\,(l)},x.\mathsf{nil},(n,f).{n}::{\mathsf{lam}\ {y}.{g}(\mathsf{tl}(f(\langle\rangle)))})}}

For this example, we consider two random walks: a classical random walk
{\sf R}_{1}:\mathsf{Nat}\to\mathsf{LazyL}
, which in each step takes a step up or down, each with probability
\frac{1}{2}
,
and a sped-up version
{\sf R}_{2}:\mathsf{Nat}\to\mathsf{LazyL}
, which stays put with probability
\frac{1}{2}
, and otherwise takes two steps either up or down.
Define these as

\displaystyle{\sf R}_{1}

\displaystyle\triangleq\mathsf{Y}(G_{{\sf R}_{1}})

\displaystyle{\sf R}_{2}

\displaystyle\triangleq\mathsf{Y}(G_{{\sf R}_{2}})

where

\displaystyle G_{{\sf R}_{1}}

\displaystyle\triangleq\mathsf{lam}\ {g}.{\mathsf{lam}\ {n}.{{n}::{\mathsf{lam}\ {y}.{\mathsf{ifz}(n,\mathsf{nil},\mathsf{choice}^{\frac{1}{2}}(g(n-1),g(n+1)))}}}}

\displaystyle G_{{\sf R}_{2}}

\displaystyle\triangleq\mathsf{lam}\ {g}.{\mathsf{lam}\ {n}.{{n}::{\mathsf{lam}\ {y}.{\mathsf{ifz}(n,\mathsf{nil},\mathsf{choice}^{\frac{1}{2}}(g(n),\mathsf{choice}^{\frac{1}{2}}(g(n-2),g(n+2))))}}}}

Intuitively,
{\sf R}_{2}
is just
{\sf R}_{1}
at double speed. The following theorem makes this intuition precise.

Theorem 9.3.

For all
n:\mathbb{N}
we have that
\mathsf{2nd}({\sf R}_{1}(\underline{2n}))\equiv_{\mathsf{Ctx}}{\sf R}_{2}(\underline{2n})
.

Proof (Sketch).

Let
V_{{\sf R}_{1}}
,
V_{{\sf R}_{2}}
and
V_{\mathsf{2nd}}
be the values that

{\sf R}_{1}
,
{\sf R}_{2}
and
\mathsf{2nd}
reduce to. It suffices to show that
V_{\mathsf{2nd}}(V_{{\sf R}_{1}}(\underline{2n}))\equiv_{\mathsf{Ctx}}V_{{\sf R}_{2}}(\underline{2n})
.
We just show
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle V_{\mathsf{2nd}}(V_{{\sf R}_{1}}(\underline{2n}))$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}{}\,\preceq^{\kappa,{\sf Tm}}_{\mathsf{LazyL}}\,{}\mathsf{eval}(V_{{\sf R}_{2}}(\underline{2n}))
by guarded recursion, omitting the
other direction, which can be proved similarly. We focus just on the case of
n>0
, which is the harder one. Unfolding definitions shows that

\displaystyle\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle V_{\mathsf{2nd}}(V_{{\sf R}_{1}}(\underline{2n}))$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}

\displaystyle=(\Delta^{\kappa})^{3}(\delta^{\kappa}(\mathsf{next}^{\kappa}({{\smash{\mathbf{inr}}}}\,{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle{\underline{2n}},{\lambda\_.W_{{\sf R}_{1}}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}})))

where

W_{{\sf R}_{1}}=\left({\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle V_{\mathsf{2nd}}(\mathsf{tl}(V_{{\sf R}_{1}}(\underline{2n\!-\!1})))$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}}\oplus^{\kappa}_{\frac{1}{2}}{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle V_{\mathsf{2nd}}(\mathsf{tl}(V_{{\sf R}_{1}}(\underline{2n\!+\!1})))$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}}\right)

Similarly,

\displaystyle\mathsf{eval}(V_{{\sf R}_{2}}(\underline{2n}))

\displaystyle\leadsto\mathsf{fold}{\,(\mathsf{inr}{\,(}\underline{2n},\mathsf{lam}\ {y}.{\mathsf{ifz}(\underline{2n},\mathsf{nil},\mathsf{choice}^{\frac{1}{2}}(V_{{\sf R}_{2}}(\underline{2n}),\mathsf{choice}^{\frac{1}{2}}(V_{{\sf R}_{2}}(\underline{2n\!-\!2}),V_{{\sf R}_{2}}(\underline{2n\!+\!2}))))}))}

So showing
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle V_{\mathsf{2nd}}(V_{{\sf R}_{1}}(\underline{2n}))$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}{}\,\preceq^{\kappa,{\sf Tm}}_{\mathsf{LazyL}}\,{}\mathsf{eval}(V_{{\sf R}_{2}}(\underline{2n}))
easily reduces to showing

(13)

\triangleright^{\kappa}(W_{{\sf R}_{1}}{}\,\preceq^{\kappa,{\sf Tm}}_{\mathsf{LazyL}}\,{}\mathsf{eval}(\mathsf{choice}^{\frac{1}{2}}(V_{{\sf R}_{2}}(\underline{2n}),\mathsf{choice}^{\frac{1}{2}}(V_{{\sf R}_{2}}(\underline{2n\!-\!2}),V_{{\sf R}_{2}}(\underline{2n\!+\!2})))))

Since
2n-1>0
, unfolding definitions gives

\displaystyle\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle V_{\mathsf{2nd}}(\mathsf{tl}(V_{{\sf R}_{1}}(\underline{2n\!-\!1})))$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}

\displaystyle=(\Delta^{\kappa})^{2}\left({\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle V_{\mathsf{2nd}}(V_{{\sf R}_{1}}(\underline{2n\!-\!2}))$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}}\oplus^{\kappa}_{\frac{1}{2}}{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle V_{\mathsf{2nd}}(V_{{\sf R}_{1}}(\underline{2n}))$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}}\right)

\displaystyle\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle V_{\mathsf{2nd}}(\mathsf{tl}(V_{{\sf R}_{1}}(\underline{2n\!+\!1})))$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}

\displaystyle=(\Delta^{\kappa})^{2}\left({\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle V_{\mathsf{2nd}}(V_{{\sf R}_{1}}(\underline{2n}))$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}}\oplus^{\kappa}_{\frac{1}{2}}{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle V_{\mathsf{2nd}}(V_{{\sf R}_{1}}(\underline{2n\!-\!2}))$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}}\right)

so by Lemma
7.7
(
13
) is equivalent to

(\triangleright^{\kappa})^{3}\left(\begin{aligned} &{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle V_{\mathsf{2nd}}(V_{{\sf R}_{1}}(\underline{2n}))$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}}\oplus^{\kappa}_{\frac{1}{2}}{\left({\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle V_{\mathsf{2nd}}(V_{{\sf R}_{1}}(\underline{2n\!-\!2}))$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}}\oplus^{\kappa}_{\frac{1}{2}}{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle V_{\mathsf{2nd}}(V_{{\sf R}_{1}}(\underline{2n\!+\!2}))$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}}\right)}\\
&{}\,\preceq^{\kappa,{\sf Tm}}_{\mathsf{LazyL}}\,{}{\mathsf{eval}(V_{{\sf R}_{2}}(\underline{2n}))}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{\frac{1}{2}}{\left({\mathsf{eval}(V_{{\sf R}_{2}}(\underline{2n\!-\!2}))}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{\frac{1}{2}}{\mathsf{eval}(V_{{\sf R}_{2}}(\underline{2n\!+\!2}))}\right)}\end{aligned}\right)

which follows from the guarded induction hypothesis and Lemma
7.8
.
∎

Remark 4.

Note again how working with denotational semantics simplifies this proof. This enables us to take two steps in the walk defined by
V_{{\sf R}_{1}}
,
collect all outcomes, and couple them with the outcomes of a single step of
V_{{\sf R}_{2}}
. Other coupling-based logics like
(
Aguirre et al., 2018
)

require specialized rules to deal with these two-to-one couplings but in our setting it is just a simple consequence of our definitions.

10.
Related Work

We have discussed some related work in the Introduction and throughout the paper; here we discuss
additional related work.

In this paper, we have shown how to use synthetic guarded domain theory (SGDT) to model
\mathsf{FPC}_{\oplus}
.
SGDT has been used in earlier work to model PCF
(
Paviotti et al., 2015
)
, a call-by-name variant of FPC
(
Møgelberg and Paviotti, 2019
)
, FPC with general references
(
Sterling et al., 2022
;
Sterling et al., 2023
)
, untyped lambda calculus with nondeterminism
(
Møgelberg and Vezzosi, 2021
)
, and guarded interaction trees
(
Frumin et al., 2024
)
.
Thus the key new challenge addressed in this paper is the modelling of probabilistic choice, in combination with recursion, which led us to introduce (guarded) convex delay algebras.

In previous works on SDGT models of PCF and FPC, the logical relation between syntax and semantics required a 1-to-1 correspondence between the steps on either side.
The previous work on nondeterminism
(
Møgelberg and Vezzosi, 2021
)
is in some ways closer to our work: To model the combination of non-determinism and recursion in the
case of may-simulation, it uses a monad defined similarly to
\mathsf{D}^{\kappa}
, but for the finite powerset monad, rather than distributions. It also uses a logical relation between syntax and
semantics, which similarly to ours is a refinement relation. The theory developed for
\mathsf{D}^{\kappa}
here, such as the
\leadsto
relation, and the lifting of relations using couplings is
new. The applications are also different:
Here we use the relation to reason about contextual refinement whereas in
op. cit.
it is used for proving congruence of an applicative simulation relation defined on operational semantics.

The use of step-indexed logical relations to model the combination of
probabilistic choice, recursive types, polymorphism and other expressive
language features has also been explored in
(
Bizjak and Birkedal, 2015
;
Aguirre and Birkedal, 2023
;
Gregersen et al., 2024
)
. These papers use an explicit
account of step-indexing, and rely on non-constructive mathematics to define
their operational semantics and to prove their soundness theorems. Our approach
is constructive, and our use of SGDT eliminates the need for explicit
manipulation of step indices. Moreover, their logical relations are defined purely
in operational terms.

Applicative bisimulation for a probabilistic, call-by-value version of PCF is studied in
(
Crubillé and Lago, 2014
)
. This work defines an approximation-based operational semantics, where a term evaluates to a family of finite distributions over values, each element of the
family corresponding to the values observed after a given finite number of steps. This is reminiscent of our semantics, although in
op. cit.
the approximation semantics is then used to define a limit semantics using suprema.

Probabilistic couplings and liftings have been a popular technique in recent years to reason about probabilistic programs
(
Barthe et al., 2009
;
Barthe et al., 2015
)
, since they provide a compositional way to lift relations from
base types to distributions over those types. To the best of our knowledge, our presentation is the first that uses constructive mathematics in the definition of
couplings and the proofs of their properties, e.g. the bind lemma. Our definition of couplings is also asymmetric to account for the fact that the distribution on the left uses guarded recursion. This is similar in spirit to the left-partial coupling definition of
(
Gregersen et al., 2024
)
, where asymmetry is used to account for step indexing.

In this paper, we model the probabilistic effects of
\mathsf{FPC}_{\oplus}
using guarded / co-inductive types. In spirit, this is similar to how effects are being modeled in both coinductive and guarded interaction trees
(
Xia et al., 2020
;
Frumin et al., 2024
)
. Coinductive interaction trees are useful for giving denotational semantics for first-order programs and are defined using ordinary type theory, whereas guarded interaction trees can also be applied to give denotational semantics for higher-order programs, but are defined using a fragment of guarded type theory.
Interaction trees have recently been extended to account for nondeterminism
(
Chappe et al., 2023
)
but, to the best of our knowledge, interaction trees have so far not been extended to account for probabilistic effects.

11.
Conclusion and Future Work

We have developed a notion of (guarded) convex delay algebras and shown how to use
it to define and relate operational and denotational semantics for
\mathsf{FPC}_{\oplus}
in
guarded type theory. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first
constructive type theoretic account of the semantics of
\mathsf{FPC}_{\oplus}
. The denotational
semantics can be viewed as a shallow embedding of
\mathsf{FPC}_{\oplus}
in constructive type
theory, which can therefore be used directly as a probabilistic programming language.
Our examples show how to use the relation between syntax and semantics for proving
contextual equivalence of
\mathsf{FPC}_{\oplus}
programs. The use of denotational semantics
for these simplifies proofs by using much fewer steps than the operational semantics.

Future work includes combining and extending the present work with the account of nondeterminism
in
(
Møgelberg and Vezzosi, 2021
)
and to compare the resulting model
with the recent classically defined operationally-based logical relation in
(
Aguirre and Birkedal, 2023
)
.

Appendix A
Omitted proofs

This appendix contains proofs omitted from the main text to be used in the review process.

Section
2

Proof of Lemma
2.1
.

The canonical map
A\to\forall\kappa.A
maps
a
to
\lambda\kappa.a
. The map defined by application
to the clock constant
\kappa_{0}
defines a left inverse to this map. It suffices to show that this is also
a right inverse, i.e., that
a\,[\kappa]=a\,[\kappa_{0}]
for all
a:\forall\kappa.A
and all
\kappa
. Since
the canonical map
B\to\forall\kappa.B
is assumed to be an equivalence, and application to
\kappa_{0}
is
a left inverse, it must also be a right inverse, so the corresponding property

b\,[\kappa]=b\,[\kappa_{0}]
for all
b:\forall\kappa.B
. Applying this to
b=\lambda\kappa.i(a\,[\kappa])

we get
i(a\,[\kappa])=i(a\,[\kappa_{0}])
, which by assumption implies
a\,[\kappa]=a\,[\kappa_{0}]
.
∎

Section
3

Proof of Lemma
3.4
.

Let
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathsf{Fin}(n))\triangleq\Sigma(f:\mathsf{Fin}(n)\to[0,1]).\mathsf{sum}(f)=1
, and note
that this carries a convex algebra structure as well as contains
\mathsf{Fin}(n)
via a dirac map
\delta
both defined in the standard way. We show that this is free by induction
on
n
. In the case of
n=0
, clearly
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathsf{Fin}(0))
is empty, and so the case follows. For the inductive
case, let
n
be the element of
\mathsf{Fin}(n+1)
not in the inclusion from
\mathsf{Fin}(n)
. Given
f:\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathsf{Fin}(n+1))
we must define
\overline{g}(f)
.
By decidability of equality for
[0,1]
, we can branch on the values of
f(n)
. If
f(n)=0
, then
f
is in the image of the obvious
inclusion from
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathsf{Fin}(n))
, and so
\overline{g}(f)
is defined by induction. If
f(n)=1
then
f=\delta(n)
and so

\overline{g}(f)
must be
g(n)
. Finally, if
f(n)=p\in(0,1)
, then
f={\delta(n)}\oplus_{p}{f^{\prime}}
for some
f^{\prime}:\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathsf{Fin}(n))
.
By induction we get
\overline{g}(f^{\prime})
and so can define
\overline{g}(f)={g(n)}\oplus_{p}{\overline{g}(f^{\prime})}
.

To prove that
\overline{g}
is a
homomorphism, suppose we are given
f:\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathsf{Fin}(n+1))
and
h:\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathsf{Fin}(n+1))
. There are nine possible cases of
f(n)
and
h(n)

and we just show the case where
f(n)=q
and
h(n)=r
. In that case
f={\delta(n)}\oplus_{q}{f^{\prime}}
and

h={\delta(n)}\oplus_{r}{h^{\prime}}
for some
f^{\prime},h^{\prime}:\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathsf{Fin}(n+1))
. From the axioms of convex algebras, one can compute, given

p,q,r:(0,1)
, probabilities
p^{\prime},q^{\prime},r^{\prime}
such that

{({a}\oplus_{q}{b})}\oplus_{p}{({c}\oplus_{r}{d})}={({a}\oplus_{q^{\prime}}{c})}\oplus_{p^{\prime}}{({b}\oplus_{r^{\prime}}{d})}

holds for all
a,b,c,d
in any convex algebra. We use this to get

\displaystyle\overline{g}({f}\oplus_{p}{h})

\displaystyle=\overline{g}({\delta(n)}\oplus_{p^{\prime}}{({f^{\prime}}\oplus_{r^{\prime}}{h^{\prime}})})

\displaystyle={g(n)}\oplus_{p^{\prime}}{\overline{g}({f^{\prime}}\oplus_{r^{\prime}}{h^{\prime}})}

\displaystyle={g(n)}\oplus_{p^{\prime}}{({\overline{g}(f^{\prime})}\oplus_{r^{\prime}}{\overline{g}(h^{\prime})})}

\displaystyle={({g(n)}\oplus_{q^{\prime}}{g(n)})}\oplus_{p^{\prime}}{({\overline{g}(f^{\prime})}\oplus_{r^{\prime}}{\overline{g}(h^{\prime})})}

\displaystyle={({g(n)}\oplus_{q}{\overline{g}(f^{\prime})})}\oplus_{p}{({g(n)}\oplus_{r}{\overline{g}(h^{\prime})})}

\displaystyle={\overline{g}(f)}\oplus_{p}{\overline{g}(h)}.

Using the induction step to conclude
\overline{g}({f^{\prime}}\oplus_{r^{\prime}}{h^{\prime}})={\overline{g}(f^{\prime})}\oplus_{r^{\prime}}{\overline{g}(h^{\prime})}
in the
third equality.
∎

In constructive mathematics, a set is said to be Kuratowski-finite if it is the codomain of a bijection from a set of the form

\mathsf{Fin}(n)
. The distributions of
\mathcal{D}(A)
satisfy a similar property.

Lemma A.1.

For any
\mu:\mathcal{D}(A)
, there exists an
n:\mathbb{N}
, a map
f:\mathsf{Fin}(n)\to A
, and a distribution
\nu:\mathcal{D}(\mathsf{Fin}(n))

such that
\mu=\mathcal{D}(f)(\nu)

Proof.

This is proved by induction on
\mu
. In the case of
\delta(a)
take
n=1
and
f
the constant map to
a
. In case
of
{\mu}\oplus_{p}{\mu^{\prime}}
we get by induction
n,n^{\prime},f:\mathsf{Fin}(n)\to A,f^{\prime}:\mathsf{Fin}(n^{\prime})\to A,\nu:\mathcal{D}(\mathsf{Fin}(n)),\nu^{\prime}:\mathcal{D}(\mathsf{Fin}(n^{\prime}))

such that
\mu=\mathcal{D}(f)(\nu)
and
\mu^{\prime}=\mathcal{D}(f)(\nu^{\prime})
. Set
m=n+n^{\prime}
such that

\mathsf{Fin}(m)\simeq\mathsf{Fin}(n)+\mathsf{Fin}(n^{\prime})
, define
\rho\triangleq\mathcal{D}(\mathsf{inl})(\nu)
,
\rho^{\prime}\triangleq\mathcal{D}(\mathsf{inr})(\nu^{\prime})

and
g:\mathsf{Fin}(m)\to A
to be the copairing of
f
and
f^{\prime}
. Then
\mathcal{D}(g)({\rho}\oplus_{p}{\rho^{\prime}})={\mu}\oplus_{p}{\mu^{\prime}}
.
∎

To prove Theorem
3.6
, we first prove a lemma:

Lemma A.2.

The types
\mathcal{D}(A+1)
and
1+[0,1)\times\mathcal{D}A
are equivalent.

Proof of Lemma
A.2
.

Using the classical definition of
\mathcal{D}
in terms of finite maps, the equivalence

\mathcal{D}(A+1)\to 1+[0,1)\times\mathcal{D}A
would map
f
to
\star
if
f(\star)=1
and otherwise
to the pair
(f(\star),\frac{1}{1-f(\star)}\cdot g)
where
g
is the restriction of
f
to
A
.
In the special case of
A=\mathsf{Fin}(n)
, by Lemma
3.4
, we can indeed define the equivalence like that and
prove it an equivalence, also in CCTT. If we do that and transport the convex algebra structure from

\mathcal{D}(\mathsf{Fin}(n+1))
to
1+[0,1)\times\mathcal{D}(\mathsf{Fin}(n))
we get a structure satisfying the following equalities.

\displaystyle{\star}\oplus_{p}{\star}

\displaystyle=\star

\displaystyle{(q,\mu)}\oplus_{p}{\star}

\displaystyle=(pq+(1-p),\mu)

\displaystyle{\star}\oplus_{p}{(r,\nu)}

\displaystyle=(p+(1-p)r,\nu)

\displaystyle{(q,\mu)}\oplus_{p}{(r,\nu)}

\displaystyle=(pq+(1-p)r,{\mu}\oplus_{\left(\frac{p(1-q)}{p(1-q)+(1-p)(1-r)}\right)}{\nu})

To prove the general statement, we will define a convex algebra on
1+[0,1)\times\mathcal{D}A

using the above equations, and prove that it defines the free convex algebra on
A+1
.

First, to see that the convex algebra operation satisfies the axioms of convex algebras,
we use the fact that it does so in the case of
A=\mathsf{Fin}(n)
. To prove transitivity, for example, there
are
8
cases to consider. One of them is

(14)

\displaystyle{\left({(r,\mu)}\oplus_{p}{(s,\nu)}\right)}\oplus_{q}{(t,\rho)}

\displaystyle={(r,\mu)}\oplus_{pq}{\left({(s,\nu)}\oplus_{\frac{q-pq}{1-pq}}{(t,\rho)}\right)}

for given
p,q,r,s,t:[0,1)
and
\mu,\nu,\rho:\mathcal{D}(A)
. By Lemma
A.1
there exists an
n
, and

\mu^{\prime},\nu^{\prime},\rho^{\prime}:\mathcal{D}(\mathsf{Fin}(n))
as well as
f:\mathsf{Fin}(n)\to A
such that
\mu=\mathcal{D}(f)(\mu^{\prime})
,

\nu=\mathcal{D}(f)(\nu^{\prime})
and
\rho=\mathcal{D}(f)(\rho^{\prime})
. (The lemma gives three different
n
,
and different functions
f
but we can find a common domain as
n+n+n
as in the proof of
Lemma
A.1
). Now, since (
14
) holds for
\mu^{\prime},\nu^{\prime},\rho^{\prime}

we can apply
[0,1)\times\mathcal{D}(f)
to both sides and get (
14
).

To see that this defines the free convex algebra structure on
A+1
,
suppose
B
is a convex algebra and
f:A+1\to B
. Let
g:\mathcal{D}A\to B
be the unique extension of
the restriction of
f
to
A
, and define the extension
\overline{f}
of
f
by the clauses

\displaystyle\overline{f}(\star)

\displaystyle\triangleq f(\star)

\displaystyle\overline{f}(p,\mu)

\displaystyle\triangleq{f(\star)}\oplus_{p}{g(\mu)}

if
p>0
and
g(\mu)
if
p=0
.
To show that this is a homomorphism, we must consider four cases. One is

\displaystyle\overline{f}({(q,\mu)}\oplus_{p}{(r,\nu)})

\displaystyle={\overline{f}(q,\mu)}\oplus_{p}{\overline{f}(r,\nu)})

where (assuming
q,r
not zero) the left hand side unfolds to

\displaystyle{f(\star)}\oplus_{pq+(1-p)r}{g({\mu}\oplus_{\left(\frac{p(1-q)}{p(1-q)+(1-p)(1-r)}\right)}{\nu})}

\displaystyle={f(\star)}\oplus_{pq+(1-p)r}{\left({g(\mu)}\oplus_{\left(\frac{p(1-q)}{p(1-q)+(1-p)(1-r)}\right)}{g(\nu)}\right)}

and the right hand side to

{({f(\star)}\oplus_{q}{g(\mu)})}\oplus_{p}{({f(\star)}\oplus_{r}{g(\nu)})}

and now the case is easily verified using the technique of
3.5
.

Finally, to show uniqueness, suppose
h:1+[0,1)\times\mathcal{D}A\to B
is another homomorphism
extending
f
. Then clearly
h(\star)=f(\star)=\overline{f}(\star)
, and since
\lambda\mu.(0,\mu)

is a homomorphism from
\mathcal{D}A
to
1+[0,1)\times\mathcal{D}A
, we also get

\displaystyle h(0,\mu)

\displaystyle=g(\mu)=\overline{f}(0,\mu)

by induction on
\mu
. So finally since
(p,\mu)={\star}\oplus_{p}{(0,\mu)}
also
h(p,\mu)=\overline{f}(p,\mu)

for any
(p,\mu)
.
∎

We can now prove the more general theorem.

Proof (sketch) of Theorem
3.6
.

Since the domain and codomain are both sets, it suffices to prove that
f
is
both surjective and injective.

To prove surjectivity, suppose
\mu:\mathcal{D}(A+B)
. We show that
\mu
is
in the image of
f
by induction on
\mu
. The case of
\mu
being a Dirac distribution is easy.
In the case of a sum
{\mu}\oplus_{p}{\nu}
, one must consider the
9
cases of the
induction hypotheses for
\mu
and
\nu
. Here we just do the case
where
\mu={\mu_{A}}\oplus_{q}{\mu_{B}}
and
\nu={\nu_{A}}\oplus_{r}{\nu_{B}}
,
omitting the
\mathcal{D}(\mathsf{inl})
and
\mathcal{D}(\mathsf{inr})
.
Let
p^{\prime},q^{\prime},r^{\prime}
be the unique elements in
(0,1)
such that

{({a}\oplus_{q}{b})}\oplus_{p}{({c}\oplus_{r}{d})}={({a}\oplus_{q^{\prime}}{c})}\oplus_{p^{\prime}}{({b}\oplus_{r^{\prime}}{d})}

holds for all
a,b,c,d
. Then

\displaystyle{({\mu_{A}}\oplus_{q^{\prime}}{\nu_{A}})}\oplus_{p^{\prime}}{({\mu_{B}}\oplus_{r^{\prime}}{\nu_{B}})}

\displaystyle={({\mu_{A}}\oplus_{q}{\mu_{B}})}\oplus_{p}{({\nu_{A}}\oplus_{r}{\nu_{B}})}

\displaystyle={\mu}\oplus_{p}{\nu}.

To prove injectivity, there are
8
cases to cover.
Suppose for example that
{\mu_{A}}\oplus_{p}{\mu_{B}}={\nu_{A}}\oplus_{q}{\nu_{B}}
for

\mu_{A},\nu_{A}:\mathcal{D}(A)
and
\mu_{B},\nu_{B}:\mathcal{D}(B)
. Then also

\displaystyle{\mu_{A}}\oplus_{p}{\delta(\star)}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{D}(A+!)({\mu_{A}}\oplus_{p}{\mu_{B}})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{D}(A+!)({\nu_{A}}\oplus_{q}{\nu_{B}})

\displaystyle={\nu_{A}}\oplus_{q}{\delta(\star)},

which via the equivalence of Lemma
A.2
allows us to conclude that
p=q
and
\mu_{A}=\nu_{A}
. Similarly, we
can prove that
\mu_{B}=\nu_{B}
. The other seven cases are similar.
∎

Alternatively, one can prove Theorem
3.6
directly by constructing a
convex algebra structure on the
3
-fold sum. Proving Lemma
A.2
first
reduces the number of cases for associativity from
27
to
8
.

Section
4

Proof of Lemma
4.10
.

(1)

By induction on
n
. For
n=0
, note that
\mathsf{run}^{n}\nu=\nu
. So the statement simplifies to
\nu\leadsto\nu
, which holds by definition.
For
n=n^{\prime}+1
, we consider the different cases for
\nu
.

•

If
\nu=\delta^{\forall\!}_{a}
, then
\mathsf{run}^{n}\nu=\nu
and the statement holds by definition.

•

If
\nu=\mathsf{step}^{\!\forall\!}\nu^{\prime}
, then we know
\nu\leadsto\nu^{\prime}
by definition of
\leadsto
. In addition,
\mathsf{run}^{n}\nu=\mathsf{run}^{n^{\prime}}\nu^{\prime}
, and we know from our IH that
\nu^{\prime}\leadsto\mathsf{run}^{n^{\prime}}\nu^{\prime}
. Hence by transitivity of
\leadsto
:
\nu\leadsto\mathsf{run}^{n}\nu
.

•

If
\nu={\nu_{1}}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{\nu_{2}}
, then
\mathsf{run}^{n}\nu={\mathsf{run}^{n}\nu_{1}}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{\mathsf{run}^{n}\nu_{2}}
, then we may assume that
\nu_{1}\leadsto\mathsf{run}^{n}\nu_{1}
and
\nu_{2}\leadsto\mathsf{run}^{n}\nu_{2}
. Then by the choice axiom of
\leadsto
also:
\nu\leadsto{\mathsf{run}^{n}\nu_{1}}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{\mathsf{run}^{n}\nu_{2}}=\mathsf{run}^{n}({\nu_{1}}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{\nu_{2}})=\mathsf{run}^{n}\nu
.

(2)

By induction on
\leadsto
.

•

For the base case
\nu=\nu^{\prime}
, the statement is trivial.

•

For
\nu=\mathsf{step}^{\!\forall\!}\nu^{\prime}
, it follows trivially that
\mathsf{run}^{0}\nu\leadsto\mathsf{run}^{0}\nu^{\prime}
. For
n\geq 1
, we have
\mathsf{run}^{n}\nu=\mathsf{run}^{n-1}\nu^{\prime}
. By the first part of this Lemma,
\mathsf{run}^{n-1}\nu^{\prime}\leadsto\mathsf{run}(\mathsf{run}^{n-1}\nu^{\prime})=\mathsf{run}^{n}\nu^{\prime}
.

•

If there is a
\nu^{\prime\prime}
such that
\nu\leadsto\nu^{\prime\prime}
and
\nu^{\prime\prime}\leadsto\nu^{\prime}
, then by induction we may assume that
\mathsf{run}^{n}\nu\leadsto\mathsf{run}^{n}\nu^{\prime\prime}
and
\mathsf{run}^{n}\nu^{\prime\prime}\leadsto\mathsf{run}^{n}\nu^{\prime}
. Then by transitivity of
\leadsto
also
\mathsf{run}^{n}\nu\leadsto\mathsf{run}^{n}\nu^{\prime}
.

•

Lastly, if
\nu={\nu_{1}}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{\nu_{2}}
and
\nu^{\prime}={\nu_{1}^{\prime}}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{\nu_{2}^{\prime}}
such that
\nu_{1}\leadsto\nu_{1}^{\prime}
and
\nu_{2}\leadsto\nu_{2}^{\prime}
, then by induction we may assume that also
\mathsf{run}^{n}\nu_{1}\leadsto\mathsf{run}^{n}\nu_{1}^{\prime}
, and similarly for
\nu_{2}
and
\nu_{2}^{\prime}
. Then also:

\displaystyle\mathsf{run}^{n}\nu

\displaystyle=\mathsf{run}^{n}{\nu_{1}}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{\nu_{2}}

\displaystyle={\mathsf{run}^{n}\nu_{1}}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{\mathsf{run}^{n}\nu_{2}}

\displaystyle\leadsto{\mathsf{run}^{n}\nu_{1}^{\prime}}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{\mathsf{run}^{n}\nu_{2}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle={\mathsf{run}^{n}\nu_{1}^{\prime}}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{\mathsf{run}^{n}\nu_{2}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{run}^{n}\nu^{\prime}

(3)

By induction on
\leadsto
.

•

The base case for
\nu^{\prime}=\nu
is trivial.

•

For
\nu=\mathsf{step}^{\!\forall\!}\nu^{\prime}
, we have that
\mathsf{run}^{1}\nu=\nu^{\prime}
, and hence
\nu^{\prime}\leadsto\mathsf{run}^{1}\nu
.

•

If there is a
\nu^{\prime\prime}
such that
\nu\leadsto\nu^{\prime\prime}
and
\nu^{\prime\prime}\leadsto\nu^{\prime}
, then by induction there is an
m_{1}
such that
\nu^{\prime\prime}\leadsto\mathsf{run}^{m_{1}}\nu
and there is an
m_{2}
such that
\nu^{\prime}\leadsto\mathsf{run}^{m_{2}}\nu^{\prime\prime}
. Then the second part of this lemma, also

\mathsf{run}^{m_{2}}\nu^{\prime\prime}\leadsto\mathsf{run}^{m_{2}}\mathsf{run}^{m_{1}}\nu=\mathsf{run}^{m_{1}+m_{2}}\nu
, and hence by transitivity:
\nu^{\prime}\leadsto\mathsf{run}^{m_{1}+m_{2}}\nu
.

•

Lastly, if
\nu={\nu_{1}}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{\nu_{2}}
and
\nu^{\prime}={\nu_{1}^{\prime}}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{\nu_{2}^{\prime}}
such that
\nu_{1}\leadsto\nu_{1}^{\prime}
and
\nu_{2}\leadsto\nu_{2}^{\prime}
, then by induction there is an
m_{1}
such that
\nu_{1}^{\prime}\leadsto\mathsf{run}^{m_{1}}\nu_{1}
and an
m_{2}
such that
\nu_{2}^{\prime}\leadsto\mathsf{run}^{m_{2}}\nu_{2}
. Let
n=max(m_{1},m_{2})
. Then by the first part of this lemma:
\nu_{1}^{\prime}\leadsto\mathsf{run}^{n}\nu_{1}
and
\nu_{2}^{\prime}\leadsto\mathsf{run}^{n}\nu_{2}
. So then also
\nu^{\prime}\leadsto\mathsf{run}^{n}\nu
.

∎

Proof of Lemma
4.13
.

By induction on
\leadsto
:

•

The first base case is obvious by reflexivity.

•

For the second base case, note that:

\overline{f}(\mathsf{step}^{\!\forall\!}\nu)=\mathsf{step}^{\!\forall\!}(\overline{f}(\nu))\leadsto\overline{f}(\nu)

•

If
\nu\leadsto\nu^{\prime}
came from the transitivity axiom, then there is a
\nu^{\prime\prime}
such that
\nu\leadsto\nu^{\prime\prime}
and
\nu^{\prime\prime}\leadsto\nu^{\prime}
. By the induction hypothesis we know that then also
\overline{f}(\nu)\leadsto\overline{f}(\nu^{\prime\prime})
and
\overline{f}(\nu^{\prime\prime})\leadsto\overline{f}(\nu^{\prime})
. Then by transitivity of
\leadsto
:
\overline{f}(\nu)\leadsto\overline{f}(\nu^{\prime})
.

•

If
\nu={\nu_{1}}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{\nu_{2}}
and
\nu^{\prime}={\nu_{1}^{\prime}}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{\nu_{2}^{\prime}}
, where
\nu_{1}\leadsto\nu_{1}^{\prime}
and
\nu_{2}\leadsto\nu_{2}^{\prime}
, then by the induction hypothesis:
\overline{f}(\nu_{1})\leadsto\overline{f}(\nu_{1}^{\prime})
and
\overline{f}(\nu_{2})\leadsto\overline{f}(\nu_{2}^{\prime})
. Then:

\displaystyle\overline{f}(\nu)

\displaystyle=\overline{f}({\nu_{1}}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{\nu_{2}})

\displaystyle={(\overline{f}(\nu_{1}))}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{(\overline{f}(\nu_{2}))}

\displaystyle\leadsto{(\overline{f}(\nu_{1}^{\prime}))}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{(\overline{f}(\nu_{2}^{\prime}))}

\displaystyle=\overline{f}({\nu_{1}^{\prime}}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{\nu_{2}^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=\overline{f}(\nu^{\prime})

∎

Proof of Lemma
4.14
.

(1)

For the first statement, we prove that
\mathsf{PT}_{n}(\nu)\leq\mathsf{PT}_{n+1}(\nu)
by induction on
n
. For
n=0
, we do a case analysis of
\nu
:

•

If
\nu=\delta^{\forall\!}a
, then by definition
\mathsf{PT}_{0}(\delta^{\forall\!}a)=1
, and
\mathsf{PT}_{1}(\delta^{\forall\!}a)=\mathsf{PT}_{0}(\mathsf{run}(\delta^{\forall\!}a))=\mathsf{PT}_{0}(\delta^{\forall\!}a)=1
. So in this case we have equality.

•

If
\nu=\mathsf{step}^{\!\forall\!}\nu^{\prime}
, then by definition
\mathsf{PT}_{0}(\mathsf{step}^{\!\forall\!}\nu^{\prime})=0
and
\mathsf{PT}_{1}(\mathsf{step}^{\!\forall\!}\nu^{\prime})=\mathsf{PT}_{0}(\mathsf{run}(\mathsf{step}^{\!\forall\!}\nu^{\prime}))=\mathsf{PT}_{0}(\nu^{\prime})\geq 0
.

•

If
\nu={\nu_{1}}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{\nu_{2}}
, then we may assume that
\mathsf{PT}_{0}(\nu_{1})\leq\mathsf{PT}_{1}(\nu_{1})
and
\mathsf{PT}_{0}(\nu_{2})\leq\mathsf{PT}_{1}(\nu_{2})
. Then also:

\displaystyle\mathsf{PT}_{0}({\nu_{1}}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{\nu_{2}})

\displaystyle={\mathsf{PT}_{0}(\nu_{1})}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{\mathsf{PT}_{0}(\nu_{2})}

\displaystyle\leq{\mathsf{PT}_{1}(\nu_{1})}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{\mathsf{PT}_{1}(\nu_{2})}

\displaystyle={\mathsf{PT}_{0}(\mathsf{run}(\nu_{1}))}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{\mathsf{PT}_{0}(\mathsf{run}(\nu_{2}))}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{PT}_{0}(\mathsf{run}({\nu_{1}}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{\nu_{2}}))

\displaystyle=\mathsf{PT}_{1}({\nu_{1}}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{\nu_{2}})

For
n=n^{\prime}+1
, notice that
\mathsf{PT}_{n+1}(\nu)=\mathsf{PT}_{n}(\mathsf{run}(\nu))
. We again go by case analysis of
\nu
:

•

If
\nu=\delta^{\forall\!}a
for some
a:A
, then
\mathsf{PT}_{n}(\delta^{\forall\!}a)=\mathsf{PT}_{n+1}(\delta^{\forall\!}a)=1
.

•

If
\nu=\mathsf{step}^{\!\forall\!}\nu^{\prime}
, then
\mathsf{PT}_{n}(\mathsf{step}^{\!\forall\!}\nu^{\prime})=\mathsf{PT}_{n^{\prime}}(\mathsf{run}(\mathsf{step}^{\!\forall\!}\nu^{\prime}))=\mathsf{PT}_{n^{\prime}}(\nu^{\prime})
, and
\mathsf{PT}_{n+1}(\mathsf{step}^{\!\forall\!}\nu^{\prime})=\mathsf{PT}_{n}(\mathsf{run}(\mathsf{step}^{\!\forall\!}\nu^{\prime}))=\mathsf{PT}_{n}(\nu^{\prime})
. By the induction hypothesis for
n
, we know that
\mathsf{PT}_{n^{\prime}}(\nu^{\prime})\leq\mathsf{PT}_{n}(\nu^{\prime})
, and hence
\mathsf{PT}_{n}(\mathsf{step}^{\!\forall\!}\nu^{\prime})\leq\mathsf{PT}_{n+1}(\mathsf{step}^{\!\forall\!}\nu^{\prime})
.

•

If
\nu={\nu_{1}}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{\nu_{2}}
, then
\mathsf{PT}_{n}(
) distributes through the sum and via a similar reasoning as above we get to the conclusion.

(2)

The second statement of this Lemma we also prove by induction on
n
. For
n=0
we go by induction on
\leadsto
.

•

If
\nu=\nu^{\prime}
, then the statement is trivially true.

•

If
\nu=\mathsf{step}^{\!\forall\!}\nu^{\prime}
, by definition
\mathsf{PT}_{0}(\nu)=0
, and hence:
\mathsf{PT}_{0}(\nu)\leq\mathsf{PT}_{0}(\nu^{\prime})
.

•

If there is a
\nu^{\prime\prime}
such that
\nu\leadsto\nu^{\prime\prime}
and
\nu^{\prime\prime}\leadsto\nu^{\prime}
, then by the induction hypothesis we have
\mathsf{PT}_{0}(\nu)\leq\mathsf{PT}_{0}(\nu^{\prime\prime})
and
\mathsf{PT}_{0}(\nu^{\prime\prime})\leq\mathsf{PT}_{0}(\nu^{\prime})
. Then by transitivity of
\leq
, we also have
\mathsf{PT}_{0}(\nu)\leq\mathsf{PT}_{0}(\nu^{\prime})
.

•

Lastly, if
\nu={\nu_{1}}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{\nu_{2}}
and
\nu^{\prime}={\nu_{1}^{\prime}}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{\nu_{2}^{\prime}}
such that
\nu_{1}\leadsto\nu_{1}^{\prime}
and
\nu_{2}\leadsto\nu_{2}^{\prime}
, then by the induction hypothesis we have
\mathsf{PT}_{0}(\nu_{1})\leq\mathsf{PT}_{0}(\nu_{1}^{\prime})
and
\mathsf{PT}_{0}(\nu_{2})\leq\mathsf{PT}_{0}(\nu_{2}^{\prime})
. Then:

\displaystyle\mathsf{PT}_{0}(\nu)

\displaystyle=p\cdot\mathsf{PT}_{0}(\nu_{1})+(1-p)\cdot\mathsf{PT}_{0}(\nu_{2})

\displaystyle\leq p\cdot\mathsf{PT}_{0}(\nu_{1}^{\prime})+(1-p)\cdot\mathsf{PT}_{0}(\nu_{2}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{PT}_{0}(\nu^{\prime}).

For
n>0
, we use that
\mathsf{PT}_{n}(\nu)=\mathsf{PT}_{0}(\mathsf{run}^{n}\nu)
. By Lemma
4.10
, we know that
\nu\leadsto\nu^{\prime}
implies
\mathsf{run}^{n}\nu\leadsto\mathsf{run}^{n}\nu^{\prime}
, and hence by the above
\mathsf{PT}_{0}(\mathsf{run}^{n}\nu)\leq\mathsf{PT}_{0}(\mathsf{run}^{n}\nu^{\prime})
, which means that
\mathsf{PT}_{n}(\nu)\leq\mathsf{PT}_{n}(\nu^{\prime})
.

(3)

The third statement is proven by induction on
\leadsto
.

•

If
\nu=\nu^{\prime}
then we use reflexivity of
=_{\mathrm{lim}}
.

•

If
\nu=\mathsf{step}^{\!\forall\!}\nu^{\prime}
. Let
n\colon\mathbb{N}
,
\varepsilon>0
. Then, by definition we have

\mathsf{PT}_{n}(\nu^{\prime})=\mathsf{PT}_{n+1}(\nu)\leq\mathsf{PT}_{n+1}(\nu)+\varepsilon
, so

\mathsf{PT}_{(.)}(\nu^{\prime})\leq_{\mathrm{lim}}\mathsf{PT}_{(.)}(\nu)
. On the other direction, by monotonicity we
have
\mathsf{PT}_{n}(\nu)\leq\mathsf{PT}_{n+1}(\nu)=\mathsf{PT}_{n}(\nu^{\prime})\leq\mathsf{PT}_{n}(\nu^{\prime})+\varepsilon
,
so
\mathsf{PT}_{(.)}(\nu)\leq_{\mathrm{lim}}\mathsf{PT}_{(.)}(\nu^{\prime})
.

•

If there is a
\nu^{\prime\prime}
such that
\nu\leadsto\nu^{\prime\prime}
and
\nu^{\prime\prime}\leadsto\nu^{\prime}
, then we apply the induction hypothesis and transitivity of
=_{\mathrm{lim}}
.

•

If
\nu={\nu_{1}}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{\nu_{2}}
,
\nu^{\prime}={\nu_{1}^{\prime}}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{\nu_{2}^{\prime}}
,
\nu_{1}\leadsto\nu^{\prime}_{1}
, and
\nu_{2}\leadsto\nu_{2}^{\prime}
, then we apply the induction hypothesis and Lemma
4.8
.

∎

Proof of Lemma
4.17
.

(1)

For reflexivity, note that
\leadsto
is reflexive and that for all
\nu:\mathsf{D}^{\!\forall\!}{A}
and all
p\in(0,1)
,
\nu={\nu}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{\nu}
. We hence have:
\nu\leadsto{\nu}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{\nu}
, proving that
\nu\leadsto^{\approx}\nu
.

(2)

For transitivity, suppose that
\nu\leadsto^{\approx}\nu_{1}
and
\nu_{1}\leadsto^{\approx}\nu_{2}
. We need to show that
\nu\leadsto^{\approx}\nu_{2}
. By definition, we have that for all
p,q\in(0,1)
there exist
\nu_{1}^{\prime},\nu_{2}^{\prime}
such that:

\displaystyle\nu

\displaystyle\leadsto{\nu_{1}}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{\nu_{1}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\nu_{1}

\displaystyle\leadsto{\nu_{2}}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{q}{\nu_{2}^{\prime}}

and so:

\displaystyle\nu

\displaystyle\leadsto{\nu_{1}}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{\nu_{1}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\leadsto{({\nu_{2}}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{q}{\nu_{2}^{\prime}})}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{\nu_{1}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\leadsto{\nu_{2}}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{pq}{({\nu_{2}^{\prime}}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{\frac{p-pq}{1-pq}}{\nu_{1}^{\prime}})}

Given any
r\in(0,1)
, we can pick
p=\frac{1+r}{2}
and
q=\frac{r}{p}=\frac{2r}{1+r}
. We then have:

\nu\leadsto{\nu_{2}}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{r}{({\nu_{2}^{\prime}}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{\frac{p-r}{1-r}}{\nu_{1}^{\prime}})},

proving that indeed,
\nu\leadsto^{\approx}\nu_{2}
.

(3)

Since
\mathsf{step}^{\!\forall\!}\nu\leadsto\nu
, we again use
\nu={\nu}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{\nu}
to show that for all
p\in(0,1)
:
\mathsf{step}^{\!\forall\!}\nu\leadsto{\nu}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{\nu}
and hence
\mathsf{step}^{\!\forall\!}\nu\leadsto^{\approx}\nu
.

(4)

Regarding probabilistic choice, suppose that
\nu_{1}\leadsto^{\approx}\nu_{1}^{\prime}
and
\nu_{2}\leadsto^{\approx}\nu_{2}^{\prime}
. Then, for all
p\in(0,1)
, we know that there exist
\nu_{1}^{\prime\prime}
and
\nu_{2}^{\prime\prime}
such that
\nu_{1}\leadsto{\nu_{1}^{\prime}}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{\nu_{1}^{\prime\prime}}
and
\nu_{1}\leadsto{\nu_{2}^{\prime}}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{\nu_{2}^{\prime\prime}}
. Then, for any
q\in(0,1)
:

\displaystyle{\nu_{1}}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{q}{\nu_{2}}

\displaystyle\leadsto{({\nu_{1}^{\prime}}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{\nu_{1}^{\prime\prime}})}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{q}{({\nu_{2}^{\prime}}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{\nu_{2}^{\prime\prime}})}

\displaystyle={({\nu_{1}^{\prime}}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{q}{\nu_{2}^{\prime}})}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{({\nu_{1}^{\prime\prime}}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{q}{\nu_{2}^{\prime\prime}})}

Hence for all
p\in(0,1)
we have a
\nu^{\prime}
(namely
{\nu_{1}^{\prime\prime}}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{q}{\nu_{2}^{\prime\prime}}
) such that
{\nu_{1}}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{q}{\nu_{2}}\leadsto{({\nu_{1}^{\prime}}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{q}{\nu_{2}^{\prime}})}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{\nu^{\prime}}
, so indeed:
{\nu_{1}}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{q}{\nu_{2}}\leadsto^{\approx}{\nu_{1}^{\prime}}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{q}{\nu_{2}^{\prime}}
.

∎

Proof of Lemma
4.18
.

We are given that
\nu\leadsto^{\approx}\nu^{\prime}
. That is, for all
p\in(0,1)
, there is a
\nu^{\prime\prime}:\mathsf{D}^{\!\forall\!}A
such that
\nu\leadsto{\nu^{\prime}}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{\nu^{\prime\prime}}
. By Lemma
4.13
, the bind operation preserves the
\leadsto
relation, and hence:

\displaystyle\overline{f}(\nu)

\displaystyle\leadsto\overline{f}({\nu^{\prime}}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{\nu^{\prime\prime}})

\displaystyle={\overline{f}(\nu^{\prime})}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{\overline{f}(\nu^{\prime\prime})},

and hence
\overline{f}(\nu)\leadsto^{\approx}\overline{f}(\nu^{\prime})
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
4.19
.

For the first statement, if
\nu\leadsto\nu_{1}
then we use that for all
p\in(0,1)
:
\nu_{1}={\nu_{1}}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{\nu_{1}}
and hence for all
p\in(0,1)
:
\nu\leadsto{\nu_{1}}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{\nu_{1}}
, proving that
\nu\leadsto^{\approx}\nu_{1}
.

For the second statement, suppose
\nu\leadsto\nu_{1}
and
\nu_{1}\leadsto^{\approx}\nu_{2}
. Then, for all
p\in(0,1)
, there exists a
\nu_{2}^{\prime}
such that
\nu_{1}\leadsto{\nu_{2}}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{\nu_{2}^{\prime}}
. So by transitivity of
\leadsto
:

\displaystyle\nu

\displaystyle\leadsto\nu_{1}

\displaystyle\leadsto{\nu_{2}}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{\nu_{2}^{\prime}},

which shows that
\nu\leadsto^{\approx}\nu_{2}
.
(Alternatively: use the first part of this lemma to conclude that
\nu\leadsto^{\approx}\nu_{1}
, and apply transitivity of
\leadsto^{\approx}
.)

For the third part, suppose
\nu\leadsto^{\approx}\nu_{1}
and
\nu_{1}\leadsto\nu_{2}
. Then, for all
p\in(0,1)
, there exists a
\nu_{1}^{\prime}
such that
\nu\leadsto{\nu_{1}}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{\nu_{1}^{\prime}}
. So by the transitivity and sum rules of
\leadsto
:

\displaystyle\nu

\displaystyle\leadsto{\nu_{1}}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{\nu_{1}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\leadsto{\nu_{2}}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{\nu_{1}^{\prime}},

which shows that
\nu\leadsto^{\approx}\nu_{2}
.
(Again, this also follows immediately from the first part of this lemma and transitivity of
\leadsto^{\approx}
.)
∎

Proof of Lemma
4.20
.

Suppose that
\nu_{1}\leadsto^{\approx}\nu_{2}
. Let us first prove that
\mathsf{PT}_{(.)}(\nu_{1})\leq_{\mathrm{lim}}\mathsf{PT}_{(.)}(\nu_{2})
.
Let
n\colon\mathbb{N}
and
\varepsilon>0
. We can assume wlog. that
\varepsilon<1
, otherwise the proof
is trivial. By definition of
\leadsto^{\approx}
,
there exists
\nu_{3}:\mathsf{D}^{\!\forall\!}A
such that
\nu_{1}\leadsto{\nu_{2}}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{(1-\varepsilon)}{\nu_{3}}
. Then, by Lemma
4.14
.
we have:

\mathsf{PT}_{n}(\nu_{1})\leq(1-\varepsilon)\cdot\mathsf{PT}_{n}(\nu_{2})+\varepsilon\cdot\mathsf{PT}_{n}(\nu_{3})\leq\mathsf{PT}_{n}(\nu_{2})+\varepsilon

Now we will prove
\mathsf{PT}_{(.)}(\nu_{2})\leq_{\mathrm{lim}}\mathsf{PT}_{(.)}(\nu_{1})
. Again, let
n\colon\mathbb{N}
and
0<\varepsilon<1
.
Then there exists
\nu_{3}:\mathsf{D}^{\!\forall\!}A
such that
\nu_{1}\leadsto{\nu_{2}}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{1-\frac{\varepsilon}{2}}{\nu_{3}}
. By Lemma
4.14

\mathsf{PT}_{(.)}({\nu_{2}}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{1-\frac{\varepsilon}{2}}{\nu_{3}})\leq_{\mathrm{lim}}\mathsf{PT}_{(.)}(\nu_{1})
, so there exists
m
such that

(1-\frac{\varepsilon}{2})\cdot\mathsf{PT}_{n}(\nu_{2})+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\cdot\mathsf{PT}_{n}(\nu_{3})\leq\mathsf{PT}_{m}(\nu_{1})+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}

And therefore,

\mathsf{PT}_{n}(\nu_{2})\leq\mathsf{PT}_{m}(\nu_{1})+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\cdot(\mathsf{PT}_{n}(\nu_{2})-\mathsf{PT}_{n}(\nu_{3}))\leq\mathsf{PT}_{m}(\nu_{1})+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}

This concludes the proof.

∎

Section

Proof of Example
5.1
.

Let

E_{V}\triangleq\lambda(\mathsf{lam}\ {x}.{M}).\mathsf{step}^{\kappa}{\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\lambda(\alpha:\kappa).\mathsf{eval}^{\kappa}(M[V/x])$}\mathclose{}{)}}}

Note that

\mathsf{eval}^{\kappa}{(\mathsf{Y}f)}=\Delta^{\kappa}(\mathsf{eval}^{\kappa}{(\mathsf{lam}\ {x}.{(e_{f}(\mathsf{fold}{\,e_{f}}))x})})

and

\mathsf{eval}^{\kappa}{(e_{f}(\mathsf{fold}{\,e_{f}}))}=(\Delta^{\kappa})^{3}(\mathsf{eval}^{\kappa}{(f(\mathsf{lam}\ {x}.{(e_{f}(\mathsf{fold}{\,e_{f}})x)})})

Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathsf{eval}^{\kappa}{((\mathsf{Y}f)(V))}=\

\displaystyle(\Delta^{\kappa})^{2}(\mathsf{eval}^{\kappa}{(e_{f}(\mathsf{fold}{\,e_{f}}))V)})

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} (\Delta^{\kappa})^{5}(\mathsf{eval}^{\kappa}{(f(\mathsf{lam}\ {x}.{(e_{f}(\mathsf{fold}{\,e_{f}})x)})}>\!>\!=^{\kappa}E_{V})\end{aligned}

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} (\Delta^{\kappa})^{5}(\delta^{\kappa}{(f)}>\!>\!=^{\kappa}\lambda(\mathsf{lam}\ {Z}.{P}).\mathsf{eval}^{\kappa}{(\mathsf{lam}\ {x}.{(e_{f}(\mathsf{fold}{\,e_{f}})x)})}\\
>\!>\!=^{\kappa}\lambda U.\Delta^{\kappa}(\mathsf{eval}^{\kappa}{(P[U/z])}>\!>\!=^{\kappa}E_{V}))\end{aligned}

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} (\Delta^{\kappa})^{4}(\delta^{\kappa}{(f)}>\!>\!=^{\kappa}\lambda(\mathsf{lam}\ {Z}.{P}).\Delta^{\kappa}.\mathsf{eval}^{\kappa}{(\mathsf{lam}\ {x}.{(e_{f}(\mathsf{fold}{\,e_{f}})x)})}\\
>\!>\!=^{\kappa}\lambda U.\Delta^{\kappa}(\mathsf{eval}^{\kappa}{(P[U/z])}>\!>\!=^{\kappa}E_{V}))\end{aligned}

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} (\Delta^{\kappa})^{4}(\delta^{\kappa}{(f)}>\!>\!=^{\kappa}\lambda(\mathsf{lam}\ {Z}.{P}).\mathsf{eval}^{\kappa}{(\mathsf{Y}f)}\\
>\!>\!=^{\kappa}\lambda U.\Delta^{\kappa}(\mathsf{eval}^{\kappa}{(P[U/z])}>\!>\!=^{\kappa}E_{V}))\end{aligned}

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle(\Delta^{\kappa})^{4}(\mathsf{eval}^{\kappa}((f(\mathsf{Y}f)))>\!>\!=^{\kappa}E_{V})

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle(\Delta^{\kappa})^{4}(\mathsf{eval}^{\kappa}{((f(\mathsf{Y}f))(V))})

This completes the proof.

∎

.

We proceed by induction on term derivations.

Case

M=x

Then

\displaystyle\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x[V/x]$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}_{\rho}=\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle V$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}_{\rho}=\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}_{\rho.x\mapsto\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle V$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\mathsf{Val},\kappa}_{\rho}}

\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{lam}\ {x}.{M^{\prime}}:{\tau_{1}}\to{\tau_{2}}

y\not=x
.

-equivalent term where this condition is true.

Proof of equation (
8
).

Note that
f
is a value by assumption and
\mathsf{Y}
as well as
e_{f}
are values by definition. Let
\rho\triangleq x\mapsto v
,
and write
\Delta^{\kappa}
for
\mathsf{step}^{\kappa}{}\circ\mathsf{next}^{\kappa}
. Let also
\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle M$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}_{\rho}\bullet\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle N$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}_{\rho}
be shorthand for

\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle M$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}_{\rho}>\!>\!=^{\kappa}\lambda f.\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle N$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}_{\rho}>\!>\!=^{\kappa}\lambda v.fv
. Then we have:

\displaystyle\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle\mathsf{Y}\,f\,x$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}_{\rho}

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle\left((\Delta^{\kappa})\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle(f(\mathsf{lam}\ {x}.{(y^{\prime}(\mathsf{fold}{\,e_{f}}))x}))$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}_{\rho,y^{\prime}\mapsto\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle e_{f}$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\mathsf{Val},\kappa}_{\rho}}\right)\bullet\ThisStyle{\mathopen{}{\llbracket}\hbox{$\SavedStyle x$}\mathclose{}{\rrbracket}}^{\kappa}_{\rho}

\displaystyle=\

Section
7

Proof of Lemma
7.3
.

The proof is organised as follows: We first show how to apply
(
Kristensen et al., 2022
, Theorem 4.3)
to encode
a coinductive type as the one described in Lemma
7.3
. Then we show that the
encoding is the same as
{}\,{}\overline{{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}}\,{}
.

Recall first the indexed version of
(
Kristensen et al., 2022
, Theorem 4.3)
: If

F:(I\to\mathsf{U})\to(I\to\mathsf{U})

is a functor in the naive sense, such that for any
X:\forall\kappa.(I\to\mathsf{U})
, the canonical map

F(\forall\kappa.X)\to\forall\kappa.F(X\,[\kappa])

is an equivalence, then a final coalgebra for
F
can be encoded by first solving
F(\triangleright^{\kappa}X)\simeq X
and then defining
the final coalgebra as
\forall\kappa.X
. Here
\forall\kappa.X
means
\lambda(i:I).\forall\kappa.(X\,[\kappa]\,i)
, and

\triangleright^{\kappa}X
means
\lambda(i:I).\triangleright^{\kappa}(X\,i)

For this application, take
I=\mathsf{D}^{\!\forall\!}(A)\times\mathsf{D}^{\!\forall\!}(A)
, and define
F(P)(\mu,\nu)
by the
clauses of Lemma
7.3
, i.e., either (spelling out the implicit inclusions)

(1)

there exists
\mu^{\prime}:\mathcal{D}{A}
such that
\mu=\mathcal{D}(\mathsf{inl})(\mu^{\prime})
,
and there is a
\nu^{\prime}:\mathcal{D}{B}
such that
\nu\leadsto^{\approx}\nu^{\prime}
, and there is a
\rho:\mathsf{Cpl}_{{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}}(\mu^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})
.

(2)

there exists
\mu^{\prime}:\mathcal{D}(\mathsf{D}^{\!\forall\!}{A})
such that
\mu=\mathcal{D}(\mathsf{inr})(\mu^{\prime})
and
P(\mathsf{run}(\mu),\nu)
.

(3)

there exist
\mu_{1}:\mathcal{D}{A},\mu_{2}:\mathcal{D}(\mathsf{D}^{\!\forall\!}{A})
, and
p:(0,1)
such that

\mu={\mathcal{D}(\mathsf{inl})(\mu_{1})}\oplus_{p}{\mathcal{D}(\mathsf{inr})(\mu_{2})}
, there exist
\nu_{1},\nu_{2}:\mathsf{D}^{\!\forall\!}{B}
such that
\nu\leadsto^{\approx}{\nu_{1}}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{\nu_{2}}
,
and
F(P)(\mathcal{D}(\mathsf{inl})(\mu_{1}),\nu_{1})
and
F(P)(\mathcal{D}(\mathsf{inr})(\mu_{2}),\nu_{2})
.

Note the use of
F(P)
in condition (3), which is due to the two recursive calls just being shorthand for (1) and (2).

Given
P:\forall\kappa.(\mathsf{D}^{\!\forall\!}(A)\times\mathsf{D}^{\!\forall\!}(A)\to\mathsf{U})
we show that
\forall\kappa.F(P\,[\kappa])\simeq F(\forall\kappa.P)
,
the right hand side of this is the statement that either

(1)

there exists
\mu^{\prime}:\mathcal{D}{A}
such that
\mu=\mathcal{D}(\mathsf{inl})(\mu^{\prime})
,
and there is a
\nu^{\prime}:\mathcal{D}{B}
such that
\nu\leadsto^{\approx}\nu^{\prime}
, and there is a
\rho:\mathsf{Cpl}_{{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}}(\mu^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})
.

(2)

there exists
\mu^{\prime}:\mathcal{D}(\mathsf{D}^{\!\forall\!}{A})
such that
\mu=\mathcal{D}(\mathsf{inr})(\mu^{\prime})
and
\forall\kappa.P\,[\kappa](\mathsf{run}(\mu),\nu)
.

(3)

there exist
\mu_{1}:\mathcal{D}{A},\mu_{2}:\mathcal{D}(\mathsf{D}^{\!\forall\!}{A})
, and
p:(0,1)
such that

\mu={\mathcal{D}(\mathsf{inl})(\mu_{1})}\oplus_{p}{\mathcal{D}(\mathsf{inr})(\mu_{2})}
, and there exist
\nu_{1},\nu_{2}:\mathsf{D}^{\!\forall\!}{B}
such that
\nu\leadsto^{\approx}{\nu_{1}}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{\nu_{2}}
,
and
F(\forall\kappa.P\,[\kappa])(\mathcal{D}(\mathsf{inl})(\mu_{1}),\nu_{1})
and
F(\forall\kappa.P\,[\kappa])(\mathcal{D}(\mathsf{inr})(\mu_{2}),\nu_{2})
.

To prove this, recall the following facts from
(
Kristensen et al., 2022
)
:
\forall\kappa.(-)
commutes with sums, products,
\Sigma
and
propositional truncation. In particular, it commutes with existential quantification over clock irrelevant types. Recall
also that our assumption of a clock constant makes all propositions clock irrelevant. In particular, in

\forall\kappa.F(P\,[\kappa])
we can commute
\forall\kappa
first over the case split, then
remove it from the case of (1) because this is a proposition not referring to
P
, and so
\kappa
will not appear in it.
In (2), we can commute
\forall\kappa
over first the existential quantification over the clock irrelevant type
\mathcal{D}(\mathsf{D}^{\!\forall\!}{A})
,
then over the product and use that
\mu=\mathcal{D}(\mathsf{inr})(\mu^{\prime})
is a proposition, arriving at condition (2) of
F(\forall\kappa.P)
.
The case of (3) is similar.

The conclusion from this is that the final coalgebra mentioned in Lemma
7.3
can be encoded as

S(\mu,\nu)\triangleq\forall\kappa.S^{\kappa}(\mu,\nu)
, where
S^{\kappa}
is the guarded recursive relation satisfying

S^{\kappa}(\mu,\nu)
iff either

(1)

there exists
\mu^{\prime}:\mathcal{D}{A}
such that
\mu=\mathcal{D}(\mathsf{inl})(\mu^{\prime})
,
and there is a
\nu^{\prime}:\mathcal{D}{B}
such that
\nu\leadsto^{\approx}\nu^{\prime}
, and there is a
\rho:\mathsf{Cpl}_{{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}}(\mu^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})
.

(2)

there exists
\mu^{\prime}:\mathcal{D}(\mathsf{D}^{\!\forall\!}{A})
such that
\mu=\mathcal{D}(\mathsf{inr})(\mu^{\prime})
and
\triangleright^{\kappa}(S^{\kappa}(\mathsf{run}(\mu),\nu))
.

(3)

there exist
\mu_{1}:\mathcal{D}{A},\mu_{2}:\mathcal{D}(\mathsf{D}^{\!\forall\!}{A})
, and
p:(0,1)
such that

\mu={\mathcal{D}(\mathsf{inl})(\mu_{1})}\oplus_{p}{\mathcal{D}(\mathsf{inr})(\mu_{2})}
, and there exist
\nu_{1},\nu_{2}:\mathsf{D}^{\!\forall\!}{B}
such that
\nu\leadsto^{\approx}{\nu_{1}}\oplus^{\!\forall\!}_{p}{\nu_{2}}
,
and
S^{\kappa}(\mathcal{D}(\mathsf{inl})(\mu_{1}),\nu_{1})
and
S^{\kappa}(\mathcal{D}(\mathsf{inr})(\mu_{2}),\nu_{2})
.

It remains to show that
S^{\kappa}(\mu,\nu)
is equivalent to
{}\,{}\overline{{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}}^{\kappa}\,{}(\mu\,[\kappa],\nu)
, which we prove by guarded recursion.
First recall that
(\mu\,[\kappa]){}\,{}\overline{{}\,\mathcal{R}\,{}}^{\kappa}\,{}\nu
iff either

(1)
